  \draw[densely dashed,black!55] (9,-.45)--(9,4.65);
  \node[align=center,font=\footnotesize] at (10,4.95) {core may\\occur here};
  \foreach \yy/\endx/\lefttext/\righttext in {
       4/4.5/{retained centers}/{$t_0-7b$},
       3/5.65/{fine density $\sigma$}/{$t_0-(7-\sqrt3)b$},
       2/6.7/{TF centers $\rho$}/{$t_0-4b$},
       1/7.45/{upper packets}/{$t_0-3b$},

    \node[anchor=east] at (-.22,\yy) {\lefttext};

- "retained centers" with a horizontal interval extending from $|x - y| = 0$ to a tick mark at $t_0 - 7b$.
- "fine density $\sigma$" with a horizontal interval extending from $|x - y| = 0$ to a tick mark at $t_0 - (7 - \sqrt{3})b$.
- "TF centers $\rho$" with a horizontal interval extending from $|x - y| = 0$ to a tick mark at $t_0 - 4b$.
- "upper packets" with a horizontal interval extending from $|x - y| = 0$ to a tick mark at $t_0 - 3b$.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### Choice of cut and the conditional field.

<a id="m:eq:screen-deleted-shell"></a>
For a candidate $t\in[5a,6a]$, let the cut transition in a shell of width $b$, using sine and cosine of an angle. Let $n_{\rm del}$ count the out particles not retained. Such a particle lies in $t-7b\le|x-y|\le t+b$. For each raw pair of particles the set of $t$ for which both lie in this shell has length at most $8b$. Averaging over $t$ therefore bounds its expected squared count by $C\beta Pm^2$, since $B(y,7a)$ has a bounded comparable-scale cover. The same averaging, applied to the nuclear integral, uses <a href="#m:eq:screen-nuclear-power" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-nuclear-power">[m:eq:screen-nuclear-power]</a> to give a bound $C\beta a^{-7}$. Averaging the sum of the two normalized nonnegative quantities selects one $t_0$ for which both bounds hold: $$\label{m:eq:screen-deleted-shell}
 \begin{gathered}
 \mathbb En_{\rm del}^2\le C\beta Pm^2,\qquad
 \mathbb En_o^2\le CPm^2,\qquad
 \mathrm{IMS}\le Cb^{-2}\sqrt Pm,\\
 \int_{\{t_0-7b\le|x-y|\le t_0+b\}}V_{\rm loc}^{5/2}
       \le C\beta a^{-7}.
 \end{gathered}$$ The full out-count and IMS bounds in the same display follow from the comparable-scale cover and $\mathbb En_z\le\sqrt Pm_z$.

<a id="m:eq:screen-field-cap"></a>
On the cut support and on $\Omega$ we have $$\label{m:eq:screen-field-cap}
 \Phi_X\le V_{\rm loc}+F,\qquad F\ge0,\qquad
 \|F\|_2\le C\frac{\sqrt Pm}{a}.$$ To see this, the raw sources outside $B(y,20a)$ give a harmonic conditional field on $B(y,10a)$; these electrons are all core particles. Its value at $v\in B(y,10a)$ is at most $\mathbb E[J_v\mid X]+Ca^{-4}$, by the same exclusion comparison as <a href="#m:eq:screen-harmonic-raw" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-harmonic-raw">[m:eq:screen-harmonic-raw]</a>. Take $F$ to be the positive supremum on $B(y,7a)$. Harmonic means and Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-positive-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-positive-field">[m:lem:screen-positive-field]</a> prove <a href="#m:eq:screen-field-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-field-cap">[m:eq:screen-field-cap]</a>. The cells of this initial cut have scale comparable to $a$ and $D_z\le C\beta^{-1}$, so <a href="#m:eq:screen-patch-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-patch-condition">[m:eq:screen-patch-condition]</a> verifies that lemma’s hypothesis.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
###### The unconstrained minimizer.

The core is at distance at least $4b$ from $\Omega$, so its potential is bounded and smooth there. Hence $\Phi_X\in L^{5/2}(\Omega)$. Young’s inequality makes <a href="#m:eq:screen-tf-functional" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-tf-functional">[m:eq:screen-tf-functional]</a> coercive in $L^{5/3}$. The positive Coulomb form is weakly lower semicontinuous: by Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-coulomb" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-coulomb">10</a> it is a squared Hilbert norm, or one may express it as the supremum of its affine dual forms. The direct method on the closed positive cone gives a minimizer, and strict convexity of the kinetic term gives uniqueness. Nonnegative variations, followed by signed compact multiplicative variations, give $$\frac53c_{\mathrm{TF}}\rho^{2/3}-\Phi_X+K*\rho\ge0,
 \qquad
 \left(\frac53c_{\mathrm{TF}}\rho^{2/3}-\Phi_X+K*\rho\right)\rho=0,$$ which is <a href="#m:eq:screen-tf-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-tf-equation">[m:eq:screen-tf-equation]</a>.

This minimizer depends continuously in $L^{5/3}$ on its field in $L^{5/2}$. Indeed convergent fields give uniformly bounded minimizers; weak lower semicontinuity and comparison with the limit minimizer identify each weak limit and give convergence of the minimum values. Lower semicontinuity of $D$ then forces convergence of the kinetic norms. Uniform convexity of $L^{5/3}$ gives strong convergence. The conditional field, being jointly measurable and taking values in the separable space $L^{5/2}(\Omega)$, is measurable as a function with values in that space. The continuity just proved establishes the stated measurability of $\rho$.

<a id="m:eq:screen-tf-mass"></a>
<a id="a:eq:screen-minimizer-mass"></a>
The diameter of $\Omega$ is at most $12a$, so $D(\rho)\ge(\int\rho)^2/(24a)$. The minimum is nonpositive because zero is admissible. Absorbing $V_{\rm loc}$ with Young’s inequality in <a href="#m:eq:screen-field-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-field-cap">[m:eq:screen-field-cap]</a> gives $$\frac{(\int\rho)^2}{Ca}\le Ca^{-7}+F\int\rho.$$ It follows that $$\label{m:eq:screen-tf-mass}
 \int\rho\le C(aF+a^{-3}),\qquad
 \int\rho^{5/3}\le C(aF^2+a^{-7}).$$ When $V_{\rm loc}=0$, the same nonpositive-minimum argument uses only $\Phi_X\le F$ and gives the sharper estimates $$\label{a:eq:screen-minimizer-mass}
 \int\rho\le CaF,\qquad \int\rho^{5/3}\le CaF^2.$$ In particular all trial errors below are integrable in $X$.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Upper quantum comparison.

<a id="m:eq:screen-patch-upper"></a>
The centers $\rho$ lie in $\Omega=B(y,t_0-4b)$; packets of width $b$ remain at distance at least $3b$ from the core. Thus Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-hole-trial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-hole-trial">14</a> applies, and radial smearing leaves the core potential unchanged. The nuclear loss is bounded at centers by $$V_b^{\rm loss}(x)=\sum_{R_m\in B(y,20a)}
             \frac{z_m}{|x-R_m|}\mathbf 1_{\{|x-R_m|\le2b\}}.$$ Weighted convexity, the total local charge bound, and integration of $|x|^{-5/2}$ over a ball of radius $2b$ give $$\int(V_b^{\rm loss})^{5/2}\le C\beta^{1/2}a^{-7}.$$ Nuclei not in the indicated local ball are harmonic on all packets. Young’s inequality now gives, pathwise, $$\label{m:eq:screen-patch-upper}
 E\le e_X+\mathcal T_X(\rho)+Cb^{-2}\int\rho
       +C\epsilon\int\rho^{5/3}
       +C\epsilon^{-3/2}\beta^{1/2}a^{-7}.$$

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### Lower quantum comparison.

<a id="m:eq:screen-patch-lower"></a>
Reserve a fraction $\epsilon T_o$ of the out kinetic energy. The coarse kinetic inequality absorbs both $V_b^{\rm loss}$ and the nuclear attraction of deleted particles, at cost $C\epsilon^{-3/2}\beta^{1/2}a^{-7}$, using <a href="#m:eq:screen-deleted-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-deleted-shell">[m:eq:screen-deleted-shell]</a>. The deleted $F$ contribution costs at most $$\|F\|_2\|n_{\rm del}\|_2\le C\sqrt\beta Pe.$$ The retained fine density $\sigma$ is supported in $\Omega$, because its centers have radius less than $t_0-7b$ and its kernel radius is $\sqrt3b$. The core potential is harmonic on these kernels, and the loss of nuclear attraction is again covered by $V_b^{\rm loss}$ at the actual particle positions. Applying the remaining fraction of Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-cubes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-cubes">15</a> and its pair-repulsion bound in the localization identity gives $$\begin{aligned}
\label{m:eq:screen-patch-lower}
 q_n[\psi]+\mathrm{IMS}
 &\ge\mathbb E\left[e_X+\mathcal T_X(\sigma)
                    -\epsilon c_{\mathrm{TF}}\int\sigma^{5/3}\right]
       -C\mathcal R,\\
 \mathcal R&=\epsilon^{-3/2}\beta^{1/2}a^{-7}
             +\sqrt\beta Pe+b^{-1}\sqrt Pm
             +b^{-2}\left(P^{2/3}m^{4/3}+\sqrt Pm\right).\notag
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\mathbb En_o^{4/3}\le(\mathbb En_o^2)^{2/3}$; no higher out-count moment is required.

<a id="m:eq:screen-tf-expansion"></a>
The Euler equation gives the exact expansion $$\label{m:eq:screen-tf-expansion}
 \mathcal T_X(\sigma)-\mathcal T_X(\rho)
       =D(\sigma-\rho)+\mathcal L+\int W_-\sigma.$$ Besides being nonnegative, the convex remainder satisfies $$\mathcal L\ge c\int\sigma^{5/3}-C\int\rho^{5/3},$$ by Young’s inequality applied to the tangent term. For small $\epsilon$ this absorbs $\epsilon c_{\mathrm{TF}}\int\sigma^{5/3}$ into half of $\mathcal L$, with error $C\epsilon\int\rho^{5/3}$. Average <a href="#m:eq:screen-patch-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-patch-upper">[m:eq:screen-patch-upper]</a>, compare it with <a href="#m:eq:screen-patch-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-patch-lower">[m:eq:screen-patch-lower]</a>, and use <a href="#m:eq:screen-tf-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-tf-mass">[m:eq:screen-tf-mass]</a>, <a href="#m:eq:screen-field-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-field-cap">[m:eq:screen-field-cap]</a>, and the IMS bound. The terms $\epsilon\mathbb E\int\rho^{5/3}$ cost $C\epsilon Pe$; the packet kinetic term costs $Cb^{-2}(\sqrt Pm+a^{-3})\le Cb^{-2}\sqrt Pm$. This proves <a href="#m:eq:screen-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-comparison">[m:eq:screen-comparison]</a>, after multiplying its constant to restore the full nonnegative remainder, and also the asserted bound for $\mathbb E\int\sigma^{5/3}$.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### The source-free improvement.

If $\nu(B(y,20a))=0$, every nucleus and the conditional core are outside the packet supports. The field $\Phi_X$ is harmonic on those supports, so radial averaging is exact and the upper comparison is <a id="a:eq:screen-upper"></a>$$\label{a:eq:screen-upper}
 E\le e_X+\mathcal T_X(\rho)+Cb^{-2}\int\rho.$$ For the lower comparison, use all the out kinetic energy. Harmonicity and the deleted-count bound give $$\sum_{i\ \mathrm{retained}}\Phi_X(x_i)=\int\Phi_X\sigma,
 \qquad \mathbb E(Fn_{\rm del})\le C\sqrt\beta Pe.$$ The full sharp kinetic and pair bounds of Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-cubes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-cubes">15</a> therefore give <a id="a:eq:screen-lower"></a>$$\label{a:eq:screen-lower}
 q_n[\psi]+\mathrm{IMS}
 \ge\mathbb E[e_X+\mathcal T_X(\sigma)]
 -C\left[\sqrt\beta Pe+b^{-1}\sqrt Pm
       +b^{-2}(P^{2/3}m^{4/3}+\sqrt Pm)\right].$$ Subtract the average upper inequality and use the exact expansion <a href="#m:eq:screen-tf-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-tf-expansion">[m:eq:screen-tf-expansion]</a>, the mass estimate <a href="#a:eq:screen-minimizer-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-minimizer-mass">[a:eq:screen-minimizer-mass]</a>, and the IMS bound. This proves <a href="#a:eq:screen-patch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-patch">[a:eq:screen-patch]</a>, including its full convex remainder, without the nuclear loss or an $\epsilon$ error.

There is a useful separate kinetic bound. In the localization identity use only $e_X\ge E$, drop pair repulsion, and use <a href="#m:eq:screen-field-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-field-cap">[m:eq:screen-field-cap]</a>. Half the coarse kinetic inequality absorbs $V_{\rm loc}$ at cost $Ca^{-7}$; also $\mathbb E(Fn_o)\le CPe$. The result is <a href="#m:eq:screen-out-kinetic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-out-kinetic">[m:eq:screen-out-kinetic]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Interior field bound.

<a id="a:eq:screen-poisson"></a>
The core vanishes in $B(y,4a)$ and this ball lies in $\Omega$. There $$\Delta W=-4\pi\nu+c_{\mathrm F}W_+^{3/2}.$$ Put $s=4a$ and $$U(x)=V_{\rm loc}(x)+\frac{C s^4}{(s^2-|x-y|^2)^4}.$$ The Laplacian of the bump is at most $80Cs^6/(s^2-|x-y|^2)^6$. For a sufficiently large universal $C$ this is at most $c_{\mathrm F}$ times its $3/2$ power. Thus $U$ is a supersolution for the displayed equation. The difference $W-V_{\rm loc}$ is bounded above: the far nuclear field is bounded on this ball, the core potential is nonnegative, and $K*\rho\ge0$. Consequently $(W-U)_+$ has compact support inside the ball. Nuclear singularities cancel in $W-U$, which is locally $H^1$; testing the difference inequality by its positive part yields $$-\int|\nabla(W-U)_+|^2
 \ge c_{\mathrm F}\int_{\{W>U\}}(W_+^{3/2}-U^{3/2})(W-U)\ge0.$$ The test is valid by compact $H^1$ approximation: the densities are locally in $L^{5/3}$, and the nuclear point terms have already canceled. Hence $W\le U$. On $B(y,3a)$ the bump is $Ca^{-4}$, proving the first part of <a href="#m:eq:screen-interior-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-interior-upper">[m:eq:screen-interior-upper]</a>. If $V_{\rm loc}=0$, this yields $W\le Ca^{-4}$; the Euler equation then gives $\rho\le Ca^{-6}$ and proves <a href="#a:eq:screen-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-interior">[a:eq:screen-interior]</a>. In this source-free case, $$\label{a:eq:screen-poisson}
 \Delta W=c_{\mathrm F}W_+^{3/2}=4\pi\rho
       \quad\hbox{on }B(y,4a).$$ Finally, weighted powers and $\nu(B(y,20a))\le a^{-3}$ give $$\int_{B(y,2a)}\rho
 \le C\int_{B(y,2a)}(V_{\rm loc}^{3/2}+a^{-6})
 \le C(a^{-9/2}a^{3/2}+a^{-3})\le Ca^{-3}.$$ ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### Closing the count estimate

The comparison in a hole bounds its electron count through a smooth Coulomb test. The resulting inequality improves any sufficiently large value of the provisional constant $P$, and therefore makes that constant universal.

<a id="stmt-m-lem-screen-count"></a>
<div id="m:lem:screen-count" class="lemma">

<a id="m:eq:screen-count"></a>
**Lemma 19** (Local second moments). *For every normalized $\delta$-state in any finite sector and every $y\in\mathcal Y$, $$\label{m:eq:screen-count}
 \mathbb En_y^2\le C\left(\max(a_y^{-6},1)+\delta a_y\right).$$ In particular the number $P$ in <a href="#m:eq:screen-count-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-count-normalization">[m:eq:screen-count-normalization]</a> is at most a universal constant.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-m-lem-screen-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-m-lem-screen-count</summary>

<a id="m:eq:screen-bootstrap-ratio"></a>
*Proof.* Fix small $\epsilon>0$ and $0<\beta<1/100$, to be chosen in that order. We need only consider $P\ge\beta^{-4}$, since $\beta$ will be fixed universally. Then $\beta^{-2}/(am)\le\sqrt P$ at every target, and the patch of Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-patch">[m:lem:screen-patch]</a> is available. Every raw particle in $B_y$ is retained, and its fine smear is contained in $B(y,1.1a)$ because $\beta<1/100$. Choose a nonnegative smooth $\chi$, equal to one there, supported in $B(y,2a)$, with $\|\nabla\chi\|_2\le C\sqrt a$. Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-coulomb" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-coulomb">10</a> and <a href="#m:eq:screen-interior-upper" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-interior-upper">[m:eq:screen-interior-upper]</a> give $$n_y\le\int\chi\sigma
 \le Ca^{-3}+C\sqrt{aD(\sigma-\rho)},\qquad
 \mathbb En_y^2\le Ca^{-6}+Ca\,\mathbb ED(\sigma-\rho).$$ Divide by $Pm^2$ and use <a href="#m:eq:screen-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-comparison">[m:eq:screen-comparison]</a>. Since $\delta a\le m^2$, the result is at most $$\begin{aligned}
\label{m:eq:screen-bootstrap-ratio}
 C\bigg[&P^{-1}+\epsilon+\sqrt\beta
       +\frac{\epsilon^{-3/2}\beta^{1/2}}P
       +\frac{\beta^{-1}}{\sqrt Pm}\\
       &+\frac{\beta^{-2}}{P^{1/3}am^{2/3}}
       +\frac{\beta^{-2}}{\sqrt Pam}\bigg].\notag
\end{aligned}$$ In the atomic distance geometry, using <a href="#a:eq:screen-patch" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-patch">[a:eq:screen-patch]</a> instead gives the sharper intermediate inequality <a id="a:eq:screen-count-bootstrap"></a>$$\label{a:eq:screen-count-bootstrap}
 \frac{\mathbb En_y^2}{Pm_y^2}
 \le C\left[P^{-1}+\sqrt\beta
 +\frac{\beta^{-1}}{\sqrt Pm_y}
 +\frac{\beta^{-2}}{P^{1/3}a_ym_y^{2/3}}
 +\frac{\beta^{-2}}{\sqrt P a_ym_y}\right].$$ For the common bound it suffices to use <a href="#m:eq:screen-bootstrap-ratio" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-bootstrap-ratio">[m:eq:screen-bootstrap-ratio]</a> in both geometries. First choose $\epsilon$ small, then $\beta$ small, and finally a universal threshold $P_0$ large. The inequalities <a href="#m:eq:screen-normalization-inequalities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-normalization-inequalities">[m:eq:screen-normalization-inequalities]</a> show that the expression is at most $1/2$ whenever $P>P_0$. Enlarge $P_0$ so that $P_0\ge\beta^{-4}$; this verifies <a href="#m:eq:screen-patch-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-patch-condition">[m:eq:screen-patch-condition]</a> at every target. If $P>P_0$, we would obtain $\mathbb En_y^2/m_y^2\le P/2$ for every $y$, contradicting the definition of $P$. Thus $P\le P_0$. Since $m_y^2\le2\max(a_y^{-6},1)+2\delta a_y$, this proves <a href="#m:eq:screen-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-count">[m:eq:screen-count]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-a-prop-screen-local"></a>
<div id="a:prop:screen-local" class="proposition">

<a id="a:eq:screen-local"></a>
**Proposition 20** (Uniform local counts and conditional fields). *In either geometry, every normalized $\delta$-state in a finite sector satisfies $P\le C$. Fix $y\in\mathcal Y$. Suppose the initial finite sequence of cuts has total out support covered by at most $C_0$ cells $B_z$, with $C_0^{-1}\le a_z/a_y\le C_0$, and satisfies the gradient hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-positive-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-positive-field">[m:lem:screen-positive-field]</a>. The absent-cut case is allowed. Then $$\label{a:eq:screen-local}
 P\le C,\qquad
 \big\|(\mathbb E[J_y\mid X])_+\big\|_2
             \le C(C_0)\frac{m_y}{a_y}.$$ Neither constant depends on the nuclear system, particle number, state, target, or $\delta$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-prop-screen-local"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-prop-screen-local</summary>

*Proof.* The count assertion is Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-count">19</a>. Comparable scales have comparable $m$ and $e=m^2/a$, uniformly in $\delta$, so $e_*\le C(C_0)e_y$ in Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-positive-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-positive-field">[m:lem:screen-positive-field]</a>. Substitute $P\le C$ into that lemma to obtain the field assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-a-cor-screen-refined"></a>
<div id="a:cor:screen-refined" class="lemma">

<a id="m:eq:screen-fine-error"></a>
<a id="m:eq:screen-density"></a>
<a id="a:eq:screen-refined"></a>
**Lemma 21** (Fine comparison and local density). *<span id="m:lem:screen-density" label="m:lem:screen-density"></span>There is a universal $a_0>0$ with the following property. If $a=a_y<a_0$ and $\psi$ is a $\delta$-state with $\delta\le a^{-7+0.02}$, choose the patch of Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-patch">[m:lem:screen-patch]</a> with $b=a^{1+0.2}$. Its densities obey $$\label{m:eq:screen-fine-error}
 \mathbb E\left[D(\sigma-\rho)+\mathcal L+\int W_-\sigma\right]
       \le Ca^{-7+0.02},\qquad
 \mathbb E\int\sigma^{5/3}\le Ca^{-7}.$$ The raw one-body density satisfies $$\label{m:eq:screen-density}
 \int_{B(y,4a)}\rho_\psi^{5/3}\le Ca^{-7}.$$ In particular, the two estimates needed in the atomic application are $$\label{a:eq:screen-refined}
 \mathbb E\left[D(\sigma-\rho)+\int W_-\sigma\right]\le Ca^{-7+.02},
 \qquad \int_{B(y,4a)}\rho_\psi^{5/3}\le Ca^{-7}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-cor-screen-refined"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-cor-screen-refined</summary>

*Proof.* Take $\beta=a^{0.2}$ and $\epsilon=a^{0.02}$. The energy assumption gives $m\le Ca^{-3}$, while $\beta^{-2}/(am)\le a^{1.6}$, so the patch hypothesis holds for small $a$. By Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-count">19</a>, $P\le C$. The terms on the right of <a href="#m:eq:screen-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-comparison">[m:eq:screen-comparison]</a> are bounded respectively by constant multiples of $$a^{-6.98},\quad a^{-6.98}+a^{-6.9},\quad
 a^{-6.93},\quad a^{-4.2},\quad a^{-6.4}+a^{-5.4}.$$ All are at most $Ca^{-7+0.02}$ for $a<1$, proving <a href="#m:eq:screen-fine-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-fine-error">[m:eq:screen-fine-error]</a>. Equation <a href="#m:eq:screen-out-kinetic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-out-kinetic">[m:eq:screen-out-kinetic]</a> gives $T_o\le Ca^{-7}$. Since the cut is full out on $B(y,4a)$, the out density equals $\rho_\psi$ there. Applying Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-kinetic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-kinetic">11</a> proves <a href="#m:eq:screen-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-density">[m:eq:screen-density]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-corollary-1"></a>
<div class="corollary">

**Corollary 22** (Raw near-Coulomb bound). *Under the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-density">[m:lem:screen-density]</a>, for $0<u\le4a$, <a id="a:eq:screen-near-coulomb"></a>$$\label{a:eq:screen-near-coulomb}
 \mathbb E\sum_{|x_i-y|<u}\frac1{|x_i-y|}
       \le Ca^{-4}(u/a)^{1/5}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-auto-corollary-1"><summary>Proof of stmt-auto-corollary-1</summary>

*Proof.* The expectation is $\int_{B(y,u)}|x-y|^{-1}\rho_\psi(x)\,dx$. Hölder’s inequality, the raw density bound, and direct radial integration give $$\int_{B(y,u)}\frac{\rho_\psi(x)}{|x-y|}\,dx
 \le\left(\int_{B(y,4a)}\rho_\psi^{5/3}\right)^{3/5}
       \left(\int_{|z|<u}|z|^{-5/2}\,dz\right)^{2/5}
 \le Ca^{-21/5}u^{1/5}.$$ This is the stated bound in either geometry. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Source-free oscillation and atomic annuli

The next estimate controls also a very negative field. It applies when the patch contains no local nuclear source; molecular patches containing nuclei instead use the offset oscillation estimate proved with the nuclear splitting below.

<a id="stmt-a-lem-screen-oscillation"></a>
<div id="a:lem:screen-oscillation" class="lemma">

<a id="a:eq:screen-oscillation"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Interior oscillation). *For every patch in Lemma <a href="#a:lem:screen-patch" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:lem:screen-patch">18</a> with $\nu(B(y,20a))=0$, and every $0<s'\le a/2$, almost surely in its data, $$\label{a:eq:screen-oscillation}
 \sup_{x\in B(y,s')}|W(x)-W(y)|
       \le C\frac{s'}a\bigl(a^{-4}+W(y)_-\bigr).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-lem-screen-oscillation"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-lem-screen-oscillation</summary>

*Proof.* By <a href="#a:eq:screen-poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-poisson">[a:eq:screen-poisson]</a> and <a href="#a:eq:screen-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-interior">[a:eq:screen-interior]</a>, $0\le\Delta W\le Ca^{-6}$ on $B(y,2a)$. Write $$Q(x)=\frac1{4\pi}
       \int_{B(y,2a)}\frac{\Delta W(z)}{|x-z|}\,\,\mathrm dz.$$ For $x\in B(y,2a)$, direct integration of $|x-z|^{-1}$ and $|x-z|^{-2}$ gives $$0\le Q(x)\le Ca^{-4},\qquad |\nabla Q(x)|\le Ca^{-5}.$$ Since $\Delta Q=-\Delta W$ inside that ball, $H=W+Q$ is harmonic there. By <a href="#a:eq:screen-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-interior">[a:eq:screen-interior]</a>, $H\le C_1a^{-4}$. Choose $C_2>C_1$ universally and put $h=C_2a^{-4}-H$, a positive harmonic function on $B(y,2a)$. The Poisson formula on a sphere of radius $3a/2$ gives $$\sup_{B(y,a)}|\nabla h|\le Ca^{-1}h(y).$$ Indeed the differentiated Poisson kernel on $B(y,a)$ is bounded by $C/a$ times its value at the center; integrating the nonnegative boundary values gives precisely this inequality. Also $h(y)\le Ca^{-4}+W(y)_-$, since $Q(y)\ge0$. Integrate the gradient along segments in $B(y,s')$, and add the bound for $\nabla Q$. This proves <a href="#a:eq:screen-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-oscillation">[a:eq:screen-oscillation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-a-cor-screen-annular"></a>
<div id="a:cor:screen-annular" class="corollary">

<a id="a:eq:screen-annular"></a>
**Corollary 24** (Annular counts). *In the atomic distance geometry, for every $0<\lambda<\Lambda<\infty$ and $u>0$, every normalized $\delta$-state of finite particle number satisfies $$\label{a:eq:screen-annular}
 \left\|\#\{i:\lambda u<|x_i|<\Lambda u\}\right\|_2
 \le C_{\lambda,\Lambda}
       \bigl(\max(u^{-3},1)+\sqrt{\delta u}\bigr).$$ All constants are independent of $Z$ and of the particle number.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-cor-screen-annular"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-cor-screen-annular</summary>

*Proof.* The compact annulus $\{\lambda\le|x|\le\Lambda\}$ has a finite cover by cells $B_z$ whose radii are comparable to one, with constants allowed to depend on $\lambda,\Lambda,L$. Dilate this cover by $u$. Because $a_{uz}=ua_z$, the dilated balls are again cells, now with radii comparable to $u$. The annular count is bounded by their sum of counts. Minkowski’s inequality and <a href="#a:eq:screen-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-local">[a:eq:screen-local]</a> bound its $L^2$ norm by $C\sum_z m_z$, which is the right side of <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a>. Boundary spheres carry zero raw probability, since the one-electron density is absolutely continuous. Thus the same estimate holds with any choice of open or closed endpoints. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:observations"></a>
### Common observation and change-of-law estimates

The two geometries use different master widths and nuclear fields, but share the observation likelihood, the cost of imposing an event, and the transfer from the observed field to a fresh patch field. We prove these statements here. In particular, the last transfer compares expectations under a specified law; it does not identify the two conditional fields on individual configurations.

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### Likelihoods and conditional versions

Let $\Psi$ be a normalized antisymmetric state in a finite $n$-electron sector. Under a probability law $P$, raw positions and spins $(x,\boldsymbol\sigma)$ have density $|\Psi|^2$. Fix finitely many widths $\ell_0,\ldots,\ell_{m-1}>0$. Independently of the raw data, take independent scalar noises $U_{ji,b}$ with density $$\zeta(u)=c_\zeta\exp\!\left(-\frac1{1-u^2}\right)
                         \mathbf 1_{(-1,1)}(u).$$ At scale $j$, form the labeled observations $\widehat Y_{ji,b}=x_{i,b}+\ell_jU_{ji,b}$ and then forget the particle labels separately in each array. Represent the result by its lexicographically sorted tuple $Y_j$ in the strict sorted chamber $\mathcal C\subset(\mathbb R^3)^n$. Ties have probability zero. Set $$\mathcal F_j=\sigma(Y_j,\ldots,Y_{m-1}),\qquad
 \mathcal F_m=\{\varnothing,\Omega\}.$$ Thus the sigma-fields decrease as $j$ increases.

Let $t_z$ be a deterministic measurable width with $t_z\ge t_{\min}>0$ for the fixed system, and use the smooth radial packet of Section <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">3</a> to define $\mathcal K_z(y)=g_{t_z}(y-z)^2$. Write $V=V_s+V_c$, where $V_s$ is the deterministic nuclear potential retained in the observed field. In the molecular application it is the smoothed nuclear potential. In the atomic application $V_s=V$ and $V_c=0$. Where relevant put $\nu_s=-(4\pi)^{-1}\Delta V_s$ distributionally; it is the smoothed nuclear measure in the first case and the original point measure in the second.

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-data"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-data" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:obs-poisson"></a>
**Lemma 25** (Conditional densities and fields). *There are jointly Borel versions of $$\mu_j(y)=\mathbb E_P\!\left[\sum_{i=1}^n\mathcal K_{x_i}(y)
                                      \mid\mathcal F_j\right],
 \qquad H_j(y)=V_s(y)-(K*\mu_j)(y),$$ with the following properties. At every positive-density data tuple, $\mu_j\ge0$, $\int\mu_j=n$, and $\mu_j$ is smooth in its spatial variable. Every spatial derivative has a deterministic bound for the fixed system. The potential $K*\mu_j$ is $C^1$, with bounded value and gradient, and $$\label{eq:obs-poisson}
 \Delta H_j=-4\pi\nu_s+4\pi\mu_j$$ in distributions. In particular, the always valid regularity statement is that $H_j-V_s$ is $C^1$ and bounded with bounded gradient. If $V_s$ and its gradient are bounded and continuous, these conclusions hold for $H_j$ itself. Conditional averaging satisfies <a id="eq:obs-tower"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-tower}
 \mathbb E_P[\mu_j(y)\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]=\mu_{j+1}(y),\qquad
 \mathbb E_P[H_j(y)\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]=H_{j+1}(y)$$ where $V_s(y)$ is finite. The same towers hold for nonnegative spatial integrals, and for integrable signed observables. These statements include the trivial data at $j=m$. None of the fixed-system regularity bounds is asserted to be uniform in the nuclear system or in $t_{\min}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-data</summary>

<a id="eq:obs-likelihood"></a>
*Proof.* On $\mathcal C$ the exact likelihood of an array is $$\label{eq:obs-likelihood}
 L_j(x,Y_j)=\sum_{\pi\in S_n}\prod_{i=1}^n\prod_{b=1}^3
 \ell_j^{-1}\zeta\!\left(
           \frac{Y_{j,\pi(i),b}-x_{i,b}}{\ell_j}\right).$$ Set it equal to zero outside the chamber. Integration of the permutation sum over $\mathcal C$ equals integration of the labeled product over all arrays, and is therefore one. Define $$\Lambda_{\ge j}(x,Y)=\prod_{h=j}^{m-1}L_h(x,Y_h),\qquad
 p_{\ge j}(Y)=\sum_{\boldsymbol\sigma}\int
       |\Psi(x,\boldsymbol\sigma)|^2\Lambda_{\ge j}(x,Y)\,\mathrm dx,$$ with the empty product and $p_{\ge m}$ both equal to one. At a tuple with $p_{\ge j}>0$, use the probability density $|\Psi|^2\Lambda_{\ge j}/p_{\ge j}$ as the conditional raw law. Equivalently, the conditional mean of a nonnegative Borel raw observable $G$ is the ratio $$\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{\boldsymbol\sigma}\int
       G(x,\boldsymbol\sigma)|\Psi(x,\boldsymbol\sigma)|^2
                      \Lambda_{\ge j}(x,Y)\,\mathrm dx}
      {p_{\ge j}(Y)}.$$ On zero-density tuples use the unconditional raw law. All these versions are jointly Borel, including their dependence on an additional spatial variable. At every parent tuple with $p_{\ge j+1}>0$, the conditional density of the next array is $p_{\ge j}/p_{\ge j+1}$. Tonelli’s theorem and $\int L_j\,\mathrm dY_j=1$ show that it integrates to one. Substitution of the ratios gives the tower property, also after nonnegative spatial integration; positive and negative parts give the integrable signed case.

For each multi-index $\alpha$, $|\partial_y^\alpha\mathcal K_z(y)|
 \le C_\alpha t_{\min}^{-3-|\alpha|}$ uniformly in $z,y$. Differentiation under the conditional integral therefore gives all claimed derivatives and bounds. Tonelli gives the mass $n$. For the potential and its gradient, split the kernels $|u|^{-1}$ and $|u|^{-2}$ at unit distance. The near parts are integrable and are controlled by $\|\mu_j\|_\infty$; the far parts are controlled by $n$. Splitting again at a small radius, then using dominated convergence on its complement, proves continuity of the potential and gradient. Finally $-\Delta K=4\pi\delta_0$ gives <a href="#eq:obs-poisson" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-poisson">[eq:obs-poisson]</a>. In the atomic case this argument does not remove the nuclear singularity of $V_s$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-30"></a>
#### Dimension-free energy cost

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-tilt"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-tilt" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:obs-tilt"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Energy cost of a data event). *Suppose $\Psi$ is an eigenstate of its sector Hamiltonian with energy $e_\Psi$, where $0\le e_\Psi-E\le D_0$. In particular, $e_\Psi=E_n$ for a sector ground state. Suppose further that $$\sum_{h=j}^{m-1}\ell_h^{-2}\le C_\ell\ell_j^{-2}$$ with a fixed numerical $C_\ell$. This holds for the dyadic scales $\ell_h=r_h^{1.01}$, $r_{h+1}=2r_h$, with $C_\ell=(1-2^{-2.02})^{-1}$. If $A\in\mathcal F_j$ has probability $p=P(A)>0$, put $p_A(x)=P(A\mid x)$ and $$\Psi_A=\sqrt{p_A(x)/p}\,\Psi.$$ Then $\Psi_A$ is a normalized antisymmetric form-domain vector, its raw position-and-spin law is the raw marginal of $P$ conditioned on $A$, and $$\label{eq:obs-tilt}
 q_n[\Psi_A]\le e_\Psi+C\ell_j^{-2}\log^5(e/p)
       \le E+D_0+C\ell_j^{-2}\log^5(e/p).$$ The constant depends only on $C_\ell$ and the chosen noise law, not on $n$, the number of arrays, or the nuclear system. The same assertion holds for an event of one unordered observation at any width $\ell$, using $\ell$ in place of $\ell_j$. No upper bound on the particle number in terms of the nuclear charge is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-tilt"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-tilt</summary>

*Proof.* In labeled observation coordinates place the sorting map inside the indicator of $A$. The translated product density is continuously differentiable in $L^1$, since $\zeta$ is smooth and compactly supported. Hence $p_A$ is continuously differentiable even for a Borel event. The scalar translation score is $$S(u)=-\frac{\zeta'(u)}{\zeta(u)}
       =\frac{2u}{(1-u^2)^2},\qquad |u|<1.$$ Its law is symmetric and centered. It satisfies $\|S(U)\|_q\le Cq^2$ for $q\ge2$: indeed, $T=(1-U^2)^{-1}$ has a finite exponential moment of every order less than one, so integration of its exponential tail gives $\|T\|_q\le Cq$, while $|S(U)|\le2T^2$.

For a unit vector $v\in\mathbb R^{3n}$ the directional derivative of the likelihood pairs the event indicator with $$Q_v=\sum_{h=j}^{m-1}\sum_{i=1}^n\sum_{b=1}^3
                    \ell_h^{-1}v_{i,b}S(U_{hi,b}).$$ Multiplication by independent random signs preserves the joint law of the independent symmetric scores. For deterministic coefficients, $$\prod_\alpha\cosh(sc_\alpha)
       \le\exp\!\left(\frac{s^2}{2}\sum_\alpha c_\alpha^2\right)$$ gives the Gaussian tail estimate and consequently $\|\sum_\alpha\epsilon_\alpha c_\alpha\|_q
 \le C\sqrt q(\sum_\alpha c_\alpha^2)^{1/2}$. Conditioning on the scores and then using the triangle inequality in $L^{q/2}$ yields $$\|Q_v\|_q\le Cq^{5/2}
       \left(\sum_{h,i,b}\ell_h^{-2}v_{i,b}^2\right)^{1/2}
       \le Cq^{5/2}\ell_j^{-1}.$$ The identity $\sum_{i,b}v_{i,b}^2=1$ and the geometric square-sum bound are precisely what avoid factors depending on dimension or on the number of observations.

Hölder’s inequality at fixed $x$ gives $|D_vp_A(x)|\le Cq^{5/2}\ell_j^{-1}p_A(x)^{1-1/q}$. For $p_A(x)>0$ choose $q=\max\{2,\log(e/p_A(x))\}$ and then take the supremum over unit vectors. At a zero the gradient of the nonnegative differentiable function $p_A$ vanishes. Therefore <a id="eq:obs-score-gradient"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-score-gradient}
 |\nabla p_A|\le C\ell_j^{-1}p_A\log^{5/2}(e/p_A),$$ with right side zero at $p_A=0$.

Regularize the square root by $\sqrt{p_A+\eta}$. Since $\sqrt u\log^{5/2}(e/u)$ is bounded on $[0,1]$, these functions have a common Lipschitz bound. Their uniform limit $\sqrt{p_A}$ is Lipschitz. Its gradient vanishes almost everywhere on its zero set, and the ordinary chain rule applies elsewhere. Thus $$\left|\nabla\sqrt{p_A/p}\right|^2
       \le C\ell_j^{-2}\frac{p_A}{p}\log^5(e/p_A).$$ The permutation sum in <a href="#eq:obs-likelihood" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-likelihood">[eq:obs-likelihood]</a> shows that $p_A$ is invariant under raw particle permutations and is spin independent. The bounded Lipschitz multiplier therefore preserves antisymmetry and the form domain. Its squared norm and its raw law are as claimed by the definition of $p_A$.

Lemma <a href="#lem:sector-multiplier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sector-multiplier">7</a>, applied with eigenvalue $e_\Psi$, now gives $$q_n[\Psi_A]-e_\Psi
    \le C\ell_j^{-2}\mathbb E_P[\log^5(e/p_A(x))\mid A].$$ Under this conditional law, $$P\{p_A(x)<e^{-u}\mid A\}
   =p^{-1}\mathbb E_P[p_A\mathbf 1_{\{p_A<e^{-u}\}}]
   \le\min\{1,e^{-u}/p\}\qquad(u\ge0).$$ Integration of this tail, split at $\log(e/p)$, bounds the fifth moment by $C\log^5(e/p)$ and proves <a href="#eq:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-tilt">[eq:obs-tilt]</a>. The proof for a single array is identical with the square-sum constant equal to one. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Transfer to a fresh patch

The following quantitative formulation isolates exactly the local screening estimates needed by both applications. It allows either a patch containing singular nuclei or a patch containing none. The fine density below is the actual sum of packets around retained raw particles, not its conditional average.

<a id="stmt-lem-obs-transfer"></a>
<div id="lem:obs-transfer" class="lemma">

**Lemma 27** (Averaged field transfer). *Fix a point $y$ where $V_s(y)$ is finite and a length $a>0$. Let $A\in\mathcal F_j$ have positive $P$-probability, and let $Q$ be $P$ conditioned on $A$, extended by fresh localization labels. Its raw marginal is the law of $\Psi_A$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-tilt">26</a>. Let $X$ denote the fresh patch data, and let $\rho_X^c$ be the conditional density of core particles under this tilted localization law. Partition the remaining raw particles into retained and deleted particles. Let $$\sigma=\sum_{i\ {\rm retained}}\vartheta_b(\cdot-x_i),\qquad
 h_X=V_s-K*\rho_X^c-K*\rho_X,\qquad W_X=V_c+h_X,$$ where $\vartheta_b$ is the radial probability kernel of Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-cubes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-cubes">15</a>, supported in $B(0,\sqrt3b)$, and $\rho_X\ge0$ is a measurably chosen patch comparison density. Assume $\rho_X$ and $\sigma$ belong to $L^{5/3}$ and have compact support for almost every patch datum. Put $t=t_y$, $\tau=t/a$, and $\beta=b/a$, and suppose $0<t,b\le a/10$. Assume the following estimates, with fixed constants:*

1.  *The master width $z\mapsto t_z$ is Lipschitz with constant at most $1/2$. The raw one-particle density $\rho_{\Psi_A}$ satisfies $$\int_{B(y,4a)}\rho_{\Psi_A}^{5/3}\le Ca^{-7}.$$*

2.  *Deleted particles have $|x_i-y|\ge ca$, and their count obeys $\mathbb E_Q n_{\rm del}\le Ca^{-3}\sqrt\beta$.*

3.  *For some $\varepsilon_a\ge0$, $$\mathbb E_Q D(\rho_X-\sigma)\le Ca^{-7}\varepsilon_a^2.$$*

4.  *On $B(y,t)$ either the source-free bound $\rho_X\le Ca^{-6}$ holds, or $$\rho_X=k(W_X)_+^{3/2},\qquad
     W_X\le V_{\rm loc}+Ca^{-4},\qquad
     V_{\rm loc}(z)=\sum_\alpha\frac{q_\alpha}{|z-R_\alpha|},
     \qquad\sum_\alpha q_\alpha\le Ca^{-3},$$ with $q_\alpha\ge0$. The latter alternative permits arbitrary locations of the local nuclei.*

<a id="eq:obs-two-towers"></a>
<a id="eq:obs-transfer"></a>
*Then the two expectation towers are $$\label{eq:obs-two-towers}
 \mathbb E_Q(K*\mu_j)(y)=\mathbb E_Q P^{\rm ms}(y),\qquad
 \mathbb E_Q(K*\rho_X^c)(y)=\mathbb E_Q P^c(y),$$ where $P^{\rm ms}$ is the potential of the raw master packets and $P^c$ is the raw core potential. Moreover, $$\label{eq:obs-transfer}
 \begin{split}
 |\mathbb E_Q(H_j-h_X)(y)|\le Ca^{-4}\big[&
    \tau^{1/5}+\beta^{1/5}+\sqrt\beta
      +\varepsilon_a\tau^{-1/2}\\
    &+R(\tau)+(\tau+\sqrt3\beta)^{1/5}\big],
 \end{split}$$ where $R(\tau)=\tau^2$ in the source-free alternative and $R(\tau)=\tau^2+\tau^{1/2}$ in the nuclear alternative. Thus the common upper bound $R(\tau)\le C\tau^{1/2}$ is always available. All constants are independent of the number of particles; they depend only on the constants in the displayed hypotheses.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-obs-transfer"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-obs-transfer</summary>

*Proof.* Write $$P^{\rm raw}(y)=\sum_iK(y-x_i),\qquad
 P^{\rm ms}(y)=\sum_i(K*\mathcal K_{x_i})(y),\qquad
 P^{\rm raw}=P^c+P^{\rm ret}+P^{\rm del}.$$ The first identity in <a href="#eq:obs-two-towers" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-two-towers">[eq:obs-two-towers]</a> follows by multiplying the original $P$-conditional expectation defining $\mu_j$ by $\mathbf 1_A$, using $A\in\mathcal F_j$, and dividing by $P(A)$. The fresh labels do not alter this expectation. For the second identity, condition the raw-plus-label marginal of $Q$ on $X$. That marginal is exactly the localization law of $\Psi_A$; the original observations can be integrated out. Tonelli justifies both identities initially for nonnegative potentials. In particular the first tower is under the original observation law, while the second uses the fresh tilted patch law.

<a id="eq:obs-transfer-decomposition"></a>
Subtracting the definitions of $H_j$ and $h_X$, and then using these towers, gives the exact algebraic identity $$\label{eq:obs-transfer-decomposition}
 \begin{split}
 \mathbb E_Q(H_j-h_X)(y)=\mathbb E_Q\big[&
 (P^{\rm raw}-P^{\rm ms})-P^{\rm del}\\
 &-(P^{\rm ret}-K*\sigma)+K*(\rho_X-\sigma)\big](y).
 \end{split}$$ The estimates below also establish finiteness of all terms, so this subtraction involves no difference of infinite expectations.

<a id="eq:obs-near-coulomb"></a>
For $0<u\le4a$, the local density bound and Hölder’s inequality give $$\label{eq:obs-near-coulomb}
 \begin{split}
 \mathbb E_Q\sum_{|x_i-y|<u}K(y-x_i)
 &\le\left(\int_{B(y,4a)}\rho_{\Psi_A}^{5/3}\right)^{3/5}
       \left(\int_{|z|<u}|z|^{-5/2}\,\mathrm dz\right)^{2/5}\\
 &\le Ca^{-4}(u/a)^{1/5}.
 \end{split}$$ The complementary raw potential is bounded by $n/(4a)$. By radial smearing, $P^{\rm raw}-P^{\rm ms}\ge0$ and only centers with $|x_i-y|\le t_{x_i}$ can contribute. Lipschitz continuity of the width implies $|x_i-y|\le t_y+|x_i-y|/2$, hence these centers lie in $B(y,2t)$. Equation <a href="#eq:obs-near-coulomb" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-near-coulomb">[eq:obs-near-coulomb]</a> bounds its mean by $Ca^{-4}\tau^{1/5}$. The same Newton comparison for each fine packet gives $$0\le\mathbb E_Q(P^{\rm ret}-K*\sigma)(y)
             \le Ca^{-4}\beta^{1/5}.$$ The distance and count assumptions for the deleted particles give $\mathbb E_Q P^{\rm del}(y)\le Ca^{-4}\sqrt\beta$.

For the signed density difference take $v_t(z)=\min\{|y-z|^{-1},t^{-1}\}$. This is in $\dot H^1$ and $$\|\nabla v_t\|_2^2=4\pi\int_t^\infty r^{-2}\,\mathrm dr=4\pi/t.$$ Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-coulomb" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-coulomb">10</a> and Cauchy–Schwarz in probability give $$\mathbb E_Q\left|\int v_t(\rho_X-\sigma)\right|
   \le Ct^{-1/2}\sqrt{\mathbb E_QD(\rho_X-\sigma)}
   \le Ca^{-4}\varepsilon_a\tau^{-1/2}.$$ It remains to control the two nonnegative remainders of the truncation inside $B(y,t)$. In the source-free case, $$\int_{B(y,t)}K(y-z)\rho_X(z)\,\mathrm dz
                         \le Ca^{-6}t^2=Ca^{-4}\tau^2.$$ For the nuclear alternative, weighted convexity gives, with $Q_{\rm loc}=\sum_\alpha q_\alpha$, $$V_{\rm loc}^{3/2}
   \le Q_{\rm loc}^{1/2}\sum_\alpha
                           q_\alpha|z-R_\alpha|^{-3/2}.$$ The assertion is read as zero if $Q_{\rm loc}=0$. Uniformly in $R\in\mathbb R^3$, $$\int_{B(y,t)}\frac{\,\mathrm dz}{|y-z|\,|z-R|^{3/2}}
                                                  \le Ct^{1/2}.$$ For completeness, apply Hölder to the second factor with any exponent $q\in(3/2,2)$ and to the first with conjugate exponent $q'\in(2,3)$. Integrals of these radial decreasing powers over a ball are no larger than their centered-ball integrals; the powers of $t$ combine to $t^{1/2}$. Since $\rho_X\le C(a^{-6}+V_{\rm loc}^{3/2})$, its remainder is at most $$C\big(a^{-6}t^2+Q_{\rm loc}^{3/2}t^{1/2}\big)
                    \le Ca^{-4}(\tau^2+\tau^{1/2}).$$ This proves the asserted nuclear bound even when a nucleus lies at $y$; only $V_s(y)$, not the unsmoothed $V(y)$, was required finite.

For $\sigma$, only packets centered within $t+\sqrt3b$ of $y$ meet the truncation ball. Extend each such packet’s kernel integral to all space and use radial smearing to bound it by its raw point potential. Equation <a href="#eq:obs-near-coulomb" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-near-coulomb">[eq:obs-near-coulomb]</a> bounds the mean remainder by $Ca^{-4}(\tau+\sqrt3\beta)^{1/5}$. The truncated $\sigma$ potential is integrable, being at most $n/t$. Together with Coulomb duality and the just obtained remainder bound, this also proves integrability of the full $\rho_X$ potential. The raw and core potentials were already integrable by <a href="#eq:obs-near-coulomb" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-near-coulomb">[eq:obs-near-coulomb]</a>. Hence all the preceding subtractions are valid. Adding the four estimates in <a href="#eq:obs-transfer-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-transfer-decomposition">[eq:obs-transfer-decomposition]</a> proves <a href="#eq:obs-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-transfer">[eq:obs-transfer]</a>. ◻

</details>

In both later applications the screening estimates supply $\varepsilon_a=a^{.01}$ and $\beta=a^{.2}$. The master geometry supplies $\tau\ge ca^w$, with $w=10^{-5}$, and an upper bound on $\tau$ tending to zero with the outer small parameter. Consequently $$a^{.01}\tau^{-1/2}\le Ca^{.01-w/2},\qquad
 .01-w/2=.009995>0,$$ and every term in the bracket in <a href="#eq:obs-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-transfer">[eq:obs-transfer]</a> tends to zero uniformly in the participating bands. Thus the transfer is $o(a^{-4})$. The specific lower probability thresholds for the events are used in those applications to verify the screening hypotheses through <a href="#eq:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-tilt">[eq:obs-tilt]</a>; no probability threshold is needed for the transfer identity itself.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### Maxima before conditional averaging

The next local rule joins fields on actual open neighborhoods. It will be used in both propagation arguments after their common singularities have been canceled.

<a id="stmt-lem-maximum"></a>
<div id="lem:maximum" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (Maximum of weak subsolutions). *Let $\mathcal O\subset\mathbb R^3$ be open, let $g$ be locally bounded, and let $F(y,t)$ be measurable in $y$, continuous in $t$, and bounded on compact spatial sets and bounded ranges of $t$. If finitely many functions $v_i\in C(\mathcal O)\cap H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathcal O)$ satisfy $$\Delta v_i\ge g+F(y,v_i)$$ in distributions, their maximum satisfies the same inequality with its own value in the reaction term. The assertion also holds for functions $v_i=V+a_i$ with continuous locally $H^1$ offsets $a_i$, when all inequalities have the common singular term $-4\pi\nu$ and the reaction vanishes in a neighborhood of $\operatorname{supp}\nu$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-maximum"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-maximum</summary>

*Proof.* It suffices to consider two functions. For a nonnegative compactly supported smooth test $\varphi$, choose an increasing Lipschitz function $\theta_\eta$ that is zero on $(-\infty,0]$ and one on $[\eta,\infty)$. Test the first inequality with $\theta_\eta(v_1-v_2)\varphi$ and the second with $(1-\theta_\eta(v_1-v_2))\varphi$. These nonnegative compactly supported $H^1$ tests are legitimate by mollification, since the right sides are bounded on their support. The sum of the left sides is $$\begin{aligned}
 &-\int\bigl[\theta_\eta(v_1-v_2)\nabla v_1
       +(1-\theta_\eta(v_1-v_2))\nabla v_2\bigr]\cdot\nabla\varphi\\
 &\hspace{8mm}
 -\int\theta_\eta'(v_1-v_2)|\nabla(v_1-v_2)|^2\varphi.
\end{aligned}$$ The second term is nonpositive. Deleting it and letting $\eta\downarrow0$ gives the gradient of $\max(v_1,v_2)$ by the Sobolev positive-part formula. Dominated convergence gives the reaction at the maximum; the reactions agree at ties. Induction proves the finite version.

For the last assertion subtract $V$ in each inequality. The singular measures cancel, and the reaction for the offsets becomes $F(y,V(y)+a_i(y))$. It is locally bounded: it vanishes near every nuclear point, and $V$ is smooth off $\operatorname{supp}\nu$. Apply the first assertion to the offsets and then add $V$ back. ◻

</details>

<a id="m:sec:void"></a>
### Electron counts in large nuclear voids

The local count bound of Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-count">19</a> controls the electrons in each annulus whose radius is comparable to its distance from the nuclei. Summing that bound over arbitrarily many annuli would lose a factor equal to the number of scales. We first remove this loss in a nuclear void. A geometric decomposition will then reduce the exterior region of an arbitrary molecule to only $O(M)$ such voids.

Throughout this section $\Psi$ is the normalized eigenstate in the largest strictly bound sector, and $E=E_N=\inf_{j\ge0}E_j$, as in Lemma <a href="#lem:sector-global-minimum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sector-global-minimum">6</a>. Expectations without an additional subscript refer to its position law, with spins summed. Constants may depend on the fixed numbers $A=40$ and $L=10^6$, but on no feature of the molecule.

<a id="stmt-m-lem-void-shell"></a>
<div id="m:lem:void-shell" class="lemma">

**Lemma 29** (A large nuclear void). *There are universal constants $t_0,C>0$ with the following property. Let $O\in\mathbb R^3$, $t\ge t_0$ and $T=2^Jt$, where $J\ge1$ is an integer. Suppose that at least one nucleus lies in $B(O,t/100)$ and that every nucleus satisfies $$|R_m-O|<t/100\qquad\text{or}\qquad |R_m-O|>100T.$$ Then <a id="m:eq:void-annulus-count"></a>$$\label{m:eq:void-annulus-count}
 \left\|\#\{i:t\le |x_i-O|<T\}\right\|_{L^2(\Psi)}\le C.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-m-lem-void-shell"><summary>Proof of stmt-m-lem-void-shell</summary>

*Proof.* Translate $O$ to the origin. Put $u_i=|x_i|$, $s_k=2^kt$ and $$n_k=\#\{i:s_k\le u_i<2s_k\},\qquad
 L_{\mathrm{ann}}=\sum_{k=0}^{J-1}n_k.$$ For $s_k/4\le |v|\le8s_k$, the nuclear assumptions give $c s_k\le d(v)\le C s_k$, where $d(v)$ is the distance to the nearest nucleus. Indeed, the inner nucleus supplies the upper bound; the inner and outer alternatives supply the lower bound. Choose $t_0$ so large that $d(v)/L>1$ on all these annuli. Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-scale">16</a> then gives $a_v=d(v)/L\asymp s_k/L$.

<a id="m:eq:void-local-shell"></a>
Each enlarged annulus is covered by a universally bounded number of local balls $B(v,a_v)$ with comparable scales. For example, take a maximal set of centers in the annulus separated by $c s_k/L$, with $c$ small; the disjoint balls of half that radius give the bound on their number. Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-count" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-count">19</a>, applied to any $\delta$-state $\omega$ in the fixed sector $N$, consequently yields $$\label{m:eq:void-local-shell}
 \|n_k\|_{L^2(\omega)}
 +\|n_{\mathrm{neigh},k}\|_{L^2(\omega)}
 \le C(1+\sqrt{\delta s_k}),
 \qquad
 n_{\mathrm{neigh},k}=\#\{i:s_k/2\le u_i\le4s_k\}.$$ This proves the result when $J$ is bounded. We may therefore assume that $J$ exceeds a fixed sufficiently large integer.

*A weighted eigenstate inequality.* Choose $0\le\chi\le1$ supported in $(32t,T/32)$, equal to one on $[64t,T/64]$, and satisfying $|\chi'(u)|\le C/u$, $|\chi''(u)|\le C/u^2$. Such a function is obtained by multiplying a fixed cutoff at scale $t$ by a fixed cutoff at scale $T$. Set $w(u)=u\chi(u)^2$. It is a bounded smooth multiplier that vanishes near the origin. For each weighted index, integration by parts gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{Re}\langle w(u_i)\Psi,-\tfrac12\Delta_i\Psi\rangle
 &=\tfrac12\int w(u_i)|\nabla_i\Psi|^2
   -\tfrac14\int \Delta_iw(u_i)|\Psi|^2\\
 &\ge-C\mathbb E\frac{\mathbf 1_{\{t\le u_i<T\}}}{u_i}.
\end{aligned}$$ These identities hold first after form-domain approximation and then by continuity, since $w$ and its derivatives are bounded for fixed $t,T$. Summing the last bound costs at most $C\sum_k s_k^{-1}\mathbb En_k\le C/t\le C$ by <a href="#m:eq:void-local-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-local-shell">[m:eq:void-local-shell]</a> with $\delta=0$. After fixing $x_i$ and its spin, the remaining Hamiltonian has energy at least $E_{N-1}\ge E$. Its contribution against the nonnegative weight $w(u_i)$ is therefore nonnegative. Testing the eigen-equation with $\sum_iw(u_i)\Psi$ shows that weighted repulsion minus attraction is at most $C$.

<a id="m:eq:void-sphere-mean"></a>
<a id="m:eq:void-weighted-field"></a>
For an electron in a shell meeting the support of $\chi$, keep only its repulsion against electrons at radius $<t$, at radius $\ge T$, and in shells $l\le k-5$. Dropping the other repulsions preserves the upper bound. Write $$P_k=\sum_{\substack{0\le l<J\\l\le k-5}}n_l,
 \qquad q=\nu(B(0,t))-\#\{i:u_i<t\},$$ and define the exterior signed potential at the origin by $$B=\int_{|R|>100T}\frac{d\nu(R)}{|R|}
       -\sum_{u_i\ge T}\frac1{u_i}.$$ For each such $k$, use a cut that is full out on $[s_k/2,4s_k]$, supported in $[s_k/4,8s_k]$, and has gradients bounded by $C/s_k$. Let $X_k$ be its out data as in Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-identity">13</a>; a bar will mean conditional expectation given $X_k$ in this paragraph and below. Set $$\varphi(v)=V(v)-\sum_{u_i<t}\frac1{|v-x_i|}
                   -\sum_{u_i\ge T}\frac1{|v-x_i|},
 \qquad
 \psi_k(v)=\overline{\varphi(v)
            -\sum_{l\le k-5}\sum_{i\in l}\frac1{|v-x_i|}}.$$ Every supported shell satisfies $32t\le s_k\le T/64$. All sources defining $\psi_k$ are outside $\{s_k/4<|v|<8s_k\}$: prefix electrons have radius $<s_k/16$, inner electrons have radius $<t\le s_k/32$, and exterior electrons have radius $\ge T\ge64s_k$. Thus $\psi_k$ has a continuous harmonic version on this annulus. Newton’s sphere-average formula gives $$\label{m:eq:void-sphere-mean}
 \langle\psi_k\rangle_u
   =\frac{\bar q-\bar P_k}{u}+\bar B,
 \qquad s_k/4<u<8s_k,$$ where $\langle F\rangle_u=(4\pi u^2)^{-1}\int_{|v|=u}F(v)\,dS(v)$. The shell-$k$ positions are all recorded in $X_k$. We may therefore condition the selected interaction terms while retaining their weights. The preceding eigenstate inequality becomes $$\label{m:eq:void-weighted-field}
 -\mathbb E\sum_k\sum_{i\in k}u_i\chi(u_i)^2\psi_k(x_i)\le C,$$ where $k$ ranges over the supported shells. Conditional harmonic versions and sphere averages are justified directly by the positive distance to these sources and their fixed finite total masses.

<a id="m:eq:void-tilt-energy"></a>
*A size-biased law for one shell.* Fix a supported $k$. Choose a smooth function $b_k$, equal to one on $[s_k,2s_k]$, supported in $(s_k/2,4s_k)$, with $0\le b_k\le1$ and $|b_k'|\le C/s_k$. Put $$S_k=\sum_i b_k(u_i)^2,\qquad m_k=\mathbb ES_k.$$ Then $n_k\le S_k\le n_{\mathrm{neigh},k}$. If $m_k=0$, every shell-$k$ contribution vanishes and can be omitted. Otherwise define $$\Psi_k=\sqrt{S_k/m_k}\,\Psi,$$ and denote its expectation and $L^2$ norm by $\mathbb E_k$ and $\|\cdot\|_{2,k}$. The square root of a sum of squares is Lipschitz, and, off its zero set, $$|\nabla\sqrt{S_k}|^2
 =\frac{\sum_i b_k(u_i)^2|\nabla b_k(u_i)|^2}{S_k}
 \le Cs_k^{-2}\mathbf 1_{\{n_{\mathrm{neigh},k}>0\}}
 \le Cs_k^{-2}n_{\mathrm{neigh},k}.$$ Its gradient is zero almost everywhere on its zero set. The multiplier identity in Lemma <a href="#lem:sector-multiplier" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:sector-multiplier">7</a> shows that $\Psi_k$ is a $\delta_k$-state, with $$\label{m:eq:void-tilt-energy}
 \delta_k\le
 \frac{C\mathbb En_{\mathrm{neigh},k}}{s_k^2m_k}.$$ The random variable $S_k$ is $X_k$-measurable, because its support is inside the full-cut zone. The joint position and cut-label law changes by the factor $S_k/m_k$. Its conditional position law given $X_k$ is therefore unchanged on $S_k>0$. In particular all the barred fields and counts above have the same conditional versions under this new law. This permits us to use the local estimates for $\Psi_k$ without changing the fields in <a href="#m:eq:void-weighted-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-weighted-field">[m:eq:void-weighted-field]</a>.

*A positive bound and a signed harmonic comparison.* On the broad annulus of harmonicity, the base and prefix sources lie outside the exclusion defining $J_v$. Its radius $Aa_v$ is a fixed small fraction of $|v|$, since $A/L$ is small. The additional electrons subtracted by $J_v$ give the upper bound <a id="m:eq:void-positive-comparison"></a>$$\label{m:eq:void-positive-comparison}
 \psi_k(v)\le\overline{J_v}
       +C\sum_{l\ge k-4}\frac{\bar n_l}{\max(s_l,s_k)}.$$ For comparable shells the exclusion supplies the denominator $c s_k$; for sufficiently larger shells radial separation supplies $c s_l$. These are precisely the electrons absent from the definition of $\psi_k$.

<a id="m:eq:void-harnack"></a>
The cut defining $X_k$ is covered by a universally bounded number of cells at scales comparable to $s_k/L$. Its gradients are $C/s_k$ and $a_v\ge1$ there, so it meets the initial-cut hypothesis of Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-positive-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-positive-field">[m:lem:screen-positive-field]</a>. For targets in the same broad annulus, that lemma and <a href="#m:eq:void-local-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-local-shell">[m:eq:void-local-shell]</a>, now applied under $\Psi_k$, give $$\|(\psi_k(v))_+\|_{2,k}
 \le \frac{C(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_k})}{s_k}.$$ Conditional Jensen’s inequality bounds $\|\bar n_l\|_{2,k}$ by $\|n_l\|_{2,k}$. Thus the added count terms are bounded by the convergent series $$C\sum_{l\ge k-4}\frac{1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_l}}{s_l}
 \le \frac{C(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_k})}{s_k}.$$ Positive parts of harmonic functions are subharmonic. Covering the compact annulus $\{s_k/2\le|v|\le3s_k\}$ by a fixed number of interior balls in the broad annulus, and using the ball submean inequality and Minkowski’s inequality, consequently gives <a id="m:eq:void-positive-supremum"></a>$$\label{m:eq:void-positive-supremum}
 A_k=s_k\sup_{s_k/2\le|v|\le3s_k}(\psi_k(v))_+,
 \qquad \|A_k\|_{2,k}\le C(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_k}).$$ The function $A_k/s_k-\psi_k$ is nonnegative and harmonic in the interior of this latter annulus. Its value at any point of a sphere $s_k\le u\le2s_k$ is at least $c$ times its sphere mean, for one universal $c>0$. Indeed, after scaling, a bounded chain of interior balls joins any two points of these spheres; on a ball, this comparison follows by comparing its Poisson kernels on a smaller concentric ball. Applying this comparison and <a href="#m:eq:void-sphere-mean" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-sphere-mean">[m:eq:void-sphere-mean]</a> gives $$\label{m:eq:void-harnack}
 u\psi_k(v)\le C A_k+c(\bar q+u\bar B-\bar P_k),
 \qquad |v|=u\in[s_k,2s_k].$$ The negative prefix term retains a fixed positive coefficient.

<a id="m:eq:void-endpoint-field"></a>
<a id="m:eq:void-final-field-bound"></a>
*The endpoint fields.* Put $s_-=2t$ and $s_+=T/2$. For a supported shell and $u\in[s_k,2s_k]$, $$\bar q+u\bar B
 =(1-\vartheta(u))(\bar q+s_-\bar B)
      +\vartheta(u)(\bar q+s_+\bar B),
 \quad
 \vartheta(u)=\frac{u-s_-}{s_+-s_-},
 \quad 0\le\vartheta(u)\le C s_k/T.$$ At either endpoint all base sources are outside the $J_v$ exclusion. Sphere averaging $\bar\varphi$ and adding back the annular electrons therefore yields $$\label{m:eq:void-endpoint-field}
 \bar q+s\bar B\le
 s\langle(\overline{J_v})_+\rangle_s
       +Cs\sum_l\frac{\bar n_l}{\max(s_l,s)},
 \qquad s\in\{s_-,s_+\}.$$ Indeed, the omitted contribution of an annular electron is nonnegative and at most its full Coulomb kernel. The latter has sphere average $1/\max(s,u_i)\le1/\max(s,s_l)$ for an electron in shell $l$. For these targets $a_v\asymp s/L$. The initial cut is still at scale $s_k/L$, so the energy parameter in Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-positive-field" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-positive-field">[m:lem:screen-positive-field]</a> satisfies $e_*\le C(\min(s,s_k)^{-1}+\delta_k)$. The precise consequence is <a id="m:eq:void-endpoint-norm"></a>$$\label{m:eq:void-endpoint-norm}
 \left\|s\langle(\overline{J_v})_+\rangle_s\right\|_{2,k}
 \le C\sqrt{\frac{s}{\min(s,s_k)}+\delta_k s}.$$ At $s=s_-$ this is at most $C(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_k})$. At $s=s_+$ the interpolation weight gives instead $$C\frac{s_k}{T}\sqrt{\frac{T}{s_k}+\delta_k T}
 \le C\left(\sqrt{\frac{s_k}{T}}
       +\sqrt{\delta_k s_k}\sqrt{\frac{s_k}{T}}\right)
 \le C(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_k}).$$ The inner endpoint count term in <a href="#m:eq:void-endpoint-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-endpoint-field">[m:eq:void-endpoint-field]</a> has norm at most $$C\sum_l2^{-l}(1+\sqrt{\delta_k2^lt})
 \le C(1+\sqrt{\delta_k t}).$$ For the outer endpoint, $s_+/\max(s_l,s_+)\le1$; we retain its contribution as $C(s_k/T)\bar L_{\mathrm{ann}}$. Thus <a href="#m:eq:void-harnack" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-harnack">[m:eq:void-harnack]</a> supplies a nonnegative, $X_k$-measurable random variable $D_k$, the same for every electron in shell $k$, such that $$\label{m:eq:void-final-field-bound}
 u_i\psi_k(x_i)\le D_k+C\frac{s_k}{T}\bar L_{\mathrm{ann}}-c\bar P_k,
 \qquad \|D_k\|_{2,k}\le C(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_k}).$$

<a id="m:eq:void-local-error-sum"></a>
<a id="m:eq:void-prefix-products"></a>
*Summation over shells.* Changing to the size-biased law and using <a href="#m:eq:void-tilt-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-tilt-energy">[m:eq:void-tilt-energy]</a>, $s_k\ge1$, and $m_k\le\mathbb En_{\mathrm{neigh},k}$, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[n_kD_k]&\le m_k\mathbb E_kD_k
 \le C m_k(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_k})\notag\\
 &\le C\left(m_k+
       \sqrt{m_k\mathbb En_{\mathrm{neigh},k}/s_k}\right)
 \le C\mathbb En_{\mathrm{neigh},k}.
 \label{m:eq:void-local-error-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ The neighbor annuli for supported $k$ lie inside $\{t\le|x|<T\}$ and have bounded overlap, so these costs sum to $C\mathbb EL_{\mathrm{ann}}$. For the outer term, $n_k$ is $X_k$-measurable and the tower property gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_k\frac{s_k}{T}\mathbb E[n_k\bar L_{\mathrm{ann}}]
 &=\sum_k\frac{s_k}{T}\mathbb E[n_kL_{\mathrm{ann}}]\\
 &\le\|L_{\mathrm{ann}}\|_2
       \sum_k\frac{s_k}{T}\|n_k\|_2
 \le C\|L_{\mathrm{ann}}\|_2.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the unconditioned norms use <a href="#m:eq:void-local-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-local-shell">[m:eq:void-local-shell]</a> with $\delta=0$, and $\sum_k s_k/T\le1$. Insert <a href="#m:eq:void-final-field-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-final-field-bound">[m:eq:void-final-field-bound]</a> into <a href="#m:eq:void-weighted-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-weighted-field">[m:eq:void-weighted-field]</a>. The weights $W_k=\sum_{i\in k}\chi(u_i)^2$ are $X_k$-measurable and satisfy $0\le W_k\le n_k$. Removing the bar on $P_k$ by the tower property, we obtain $$\label{m:eq:void-prefix-products}
 \mathbb E\sum_kW_kP_k\le C(1+\|L_{\mathrm{ann}}\|_2).$$

Let $I_{\mathrm{mid}}$ consist of the shells entirely contained in $[64t,T/64]$, and set $L_{\mathrm{mid}}=\sum_{k\in I_{\mathrm{mid}}}n_k$. On these shells $W_k=n_k$. The sum on the left of <a href="#m:eq:void-prefix-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-prefix-products">[m:eq:void-prefix-products]</a> therefore includes all products $n_kn_l$ with $k,l\in I_{\mathrm{mid}}$ and $l\le k-5$. For every omitted close pair $|k-l|<5$, including $k=l$, use the tilt at $k$ and <a href="#m:eq:void-local-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-local-shell">[m:eq:void-local-shell]</a> to obtain $$\mathbb En_kn_l\le m_k\mathbb E_kn_l
 \le C m_k(1+\sqrt{\delta_k s_l})
 \le C\mathbb En_{\mathrm{neigh},k}.$$ The last step uses $s_l\asymp s_k$ and the same calculation as <a href="#m:eq:void-local-error-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-local-error-sum">[m:eq:void-local-error-sum]</a>. Each $k$ has only a bounded number of such neighbors. Thus the close-pair costs sum to $C\mathbb EL_{\mathrm{ann}}$, rather than a cost proportional to $J$. Expanding $L_{\mathrm{mid}}^2$ now gives $$\mathbb EL_{\mathrm{mid}}^2\le C(1+\|L_{\mathrm{ann}}\|_2).$$ Only a fixed number of endpoint shells were omitted, so $\|L_{\mathrm{ann}}-L_{\mathrm{mid}}\|_2\le C$ by <a href="#m:eq:void-local-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-local-shell">[m:eq:void-local-shell]</a>. Consequently $X=\|L_{\mathrm{ann}}\|_2$ satisfies $X^2\le C(1+X)$, proving the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-m-lem-void-count"></a>
<div id="m:lem:void-count" class="lemma">

<a id="m:eq:void-count"></a>
**Lemma 30** (Exterior count). *There is a universal constant $C$ such that, for $0<s\le 1$, $$\label{m:eq:void-count}
 \mathbb E\#\{i:a_{x_i}\ge s\}\le CMs^{-3}.$$ The constant is independent of the charges and the distances between nuclei.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-m-lem-void-count"><summary>Proof of stmt-m-lem-void-count</summary>

<a id="m:eq:void-net-local-count"></a>
<a id="m:eq:void-net-far-count"></a>
*Proof.* Write $\mathcal R=\{R_1,\ldots,R_M\}$ and $d(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\mathcal R)$. We use $d(x)\le L a_x$ from Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-scale">16</a>. For a Borel set $B$, write $n(B)=\#\{i:x_i\in B\}$. The local count bound <a href="#m:eq:screen-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-count">[m:eq:screen-count]</a>, applied with $\delta=0$, gives $$\label{m:eq:void-net-local-count}
 \mathbb En(B(y,a_y))
 \le C\max\{a_y^{-3},1\}.$$ We first prove that $$\label{m:eq:void-net-far-count}
 \mathbb En(\{d\ge U\})\le CM$$ for a sufficiently large universal constant $U$. A direct summation of local bounds over all large distance scales would give an infinite series. We instead group scales into a bounded number of annuli to which Lemma <a href="#m:lem:void-shell" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:void-shell">29</a> applies.

<a id="pm-h-34"></a>
###### Nested sets of nuclear representatives.

Fix $$h=12,\qquad \varepsilon=2^{1-h}=\frac1{2048},\qquad
 D_0=1000,\qquad q=10.$$ Thus $$\varepsilon<\frac1{200},\qquad D_0-\varepsilon>400,
 \qquad D_0>3\varepsilon,\qquad 2^q>D_0.$$ Choose an integer $j_0$ such that $2^{j_0}/L\ge1$ and $2^{j_0-1}$ exceeds the lower threshold for the inner radius in Lemma <a href="#m:lem:void-shell" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:void-shell">29</a>. Set $U=2^{j_0}$. Since the nuclei are distinct, there is an integer $l_0<j_0-h$ such that $\mathcal R$ is $2^{l_0}$-separated. Put $P_{l_0}=\mathcal R$. For each $l>l_0$, choose a maximal $2^l$-separated subset $P_l\subset P_{l-1}$, where separated means that distinct points have distance at least $2^l$.

<a id="m:eq:void-net-ancestor-offset"></a>
For each $p\in P_{l-1}$ choose a parent $\pi_l(p)\in P_l$, keeping $\pi_l(p)=p$ for $p\in P_l$. Maximality permits this choice with $|p-\pi_l(p)|<2^l$ whenever $p$ is removed. Composing these maps gives an ancestor $\alpha_l(R)\in P_l$ for every original nucleus $R$, and $$\label{m:eq:void-net-ancestor-offset}
 |R-\alpha_l(R)|<2^{l+1}.$$ Indeed, the accumulated displacement is less than $\sum_{k=l_0+1}^l2^k<2^{l+1}$; at $l=l_0$ it is zero. Every representative is an original nucleus, and a removed nucleus never reappears in the nested sets.

<a id="m:eq:void-net-assigned-set"></a>
For $j\ge j_0$, put $u_j=2^j$. Choose a nearest nucleus $R(x)$ measurably, breaking ties by the least index. Assign a point of the dyad $u_j\le d(x)<2u_j$ to $c=\alpha_{j-h}(R(x))\in P_{j-h}$, and denote its assigned set by $A_{j,c}$. These are Borel sets, disjoint at each scale, and $$\label{m:eq:void-net-assigned-set}
 |R(x)-c|<\varepsilon u_j,\qquad
 A_{j,c}\subset B(c,(2+\varepsilon)u_j).$$

<a id="pm-h-35"></a>
###### Only finitely many crowded pairs.

Call $(j,c)$ crowded if there is a distinct $w\in P_{j-h}$ with $|w-c|\le D_0u_j$; otherwise call it isolated. We claim that the number of crowded pairs is at most $CM$. For each crowded pair choose one witness $w$. The points $c$ and $w$ cannot both survive to $P_{j+q}$, since that set is $2^{j+q}$-separated and $2^{j+q}>D_0u_j$. Let $r$ be the first level at which either endpoint is removed, and charge $(j,c)$ to one such removal event $(r,p)$, where $p\in P_{r-1}\setminus P_r$. Then <a id="m:eq:void-net-charge-window"></a>$$\label{m:eq:void-net-charge-window}
 j-h<r\le j+q,
 \qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad
 r-q\le j\le r+h-1.$$

<a id="m:eq:void-net-crowded-number"></a>
A fixed removal event can therefore receive charges from at most $q+h$ levels $j$. At any one of these levels, a center $c$ charging $(r,p)$ is either $p$ itself or has $p$ as its witness. In either case, $c\in P_{j-h}\cap\overline B(p,D_0u_j)$. Such centers are $2^{j-h}$-separated. The disjoint balls of radius $2^{j-h-1}$ around them lie in the ball of radius $D_0u_j+2^{j-h-1}$ around $p$, so their number is at most $(1+2D_0 2^h)^3$. This is a fixed constant. There are at most $M-1$ removal events, since the nested sets eventually contain one nucleus. Consequently $$\label{m:eq:void-net-crowded-number}
 \#\{(j,c):j\ge j_0,\ (j,c)\text{ is crowded}\}\le CM.$$ Simultaneous removals cause no difficulty: the charge uses just one of the two removed endpoints.

<a id="m:eq:void-net-assigned-count"></a>
Each crowded assigned set has bounded expected particle count. To see this, choose a maximal $u_j/(2L)$-separated subset of $A_{j,c}$. By <a href="#m:eq:void-net-assigned-set" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-assigned-set">[m:eq:void-net-assigned-set]</a>, a volume comparison bounds its cardinality by $CL^3$. Its balls of radius $u_j/(2L)$ cover $A_{j,c}$. At every center $y$ we have $a_y\ge d(y)/L\ge u_j/L\ge1$, so these covering balls are contained in $B(y,a_y)$. Equation <a href="#m:eq:void-net-local-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-local-count">[m:eq:void-net-local-count]</a> therefore gives $$\label{m:eq:void-net-assigned-count}
 \mathbb En(A_{j,c})\le C.$$ Together, <a href="#m:eq:void-net-crowded-number" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-crowded-number">[m:eq:void-net-crowded-number]</a> and <a href="#m:eq:void-net-assigned-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-assigned-count">[m:eq:void-net-assigned-count]</a> show that all crowded assigned sets cost at most $CM$ expected particles.

<a id="pm-h-36"></a>
###### Isolated runs give empty annuli.

For a fixed nucleus $c$, the levels $j\ge j_0$ at which $c\in P_{j-h}$ form an initial integer interval, possibly empty or infinite. Removing its crowded levels leaves consecutive runs of isolated levels. The number of these runs is at most one plus the number of crowded levels for $c$. Summing over $c$ and using <a href="#m:eq:void-net-crowded-number" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-crowded-number">[m:eq:void-net-crowded-number]</a>, the total number of isolated runs is at most $CM$.

<a id="m:eq:void-net-cluster-dichotomy"></a>
At an isolated level $j$ for $c$, every original nucleus $R$ satisfies one of the two alternatives $$\label{m:eq:void-net-cluster-dichotomy}
 |R-c|<\varepsilon u_j
 \qquad\text{or}\qquad
 |R-c|>(D_0-\varepsilon)u_j.$$ Indeed, if $\alpha_{j-h}(R)=c$, the first alternative follows from <a href="#m:eq:void-net-ancestor-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-ancestor-offset">[m:eq:void-net-ancestor-offset]</a>. Otherwise isolation gives $|\alpha_{j-h}(R)-c|>D_0u_j$, and the ancestor offset gives the second alternative.

The alternative for a fixed nucleus cannot change between consecutive isolated levels $j$ and $j+1$ for the same center $c$. A nucleus near $c$ at level $j$ remains too close to satisfy the far alternative at $j+1$. Conversely, a nucleus far at $j$ could be near at $j+1$ only if its fixed distance from $c$ were both greater than $(D_0-\varepsilon)u_j$ and less than $2\varepsilon u_j$, which is impossible because $D_0>3\varepsilon$. Thus the set of near nuclei is constant throughout each isolated run. This argument permits ancestor maps to change and uses only the gap in <a href="#m:eq:void-net-cluster-dichotomy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-cluster-dichotomy">[m:eq:void-net-cluster-dichotomy]</a>.

Consider first a finite run $j=a,\ldots,b$ and put $$O=c,\qquad t=u_a/2,\qquad T=4u_b.$$ The near nuclei have distance less than $\varepsilon u_a<t/100$ from $c$. All other nuclei have distance greater than $(D_0-\varepsilon)u_b>100T$. There is a nucleus at $c$, and $T/t=2^{b-a+3}$ is a power of two. By the choice of $j_0$, Lemma <a href="#m:lem:void-shell" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:void-shell">29</a> applies. Every assigned point from this run belongs to its annulus, since $c$ is itself a nucleus and $$t\le u_a\le d(x)\le |x-c|<(2+\varepsilon)u_b<T.$$ It follows that the union of the assigned sets over this run has expected particle count at most $C$, independently of its length. For an infinite run, apply the same argument to each finite initial segment and then use monotone convergence for their increasing assigned unions. There are at most $CM$ runs, so the total contribution of all isolated assigned sets is at most $CM$. Combined with the crowded sets, this proves <a href="#m:eq:void-net-far-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-far-count">[m:eq:void-net-far-count]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-37"></a>
###### The remaining distances.

It remains to count particles with $a_x\ge s$ and $d(x)<U$. For the part with $d(x)<s$, choose a maximal $s/2$-separated subset of $\{d<s,\ a\ge s\}$. Its centers lie in $\bigcup_{m=1}^M B(R_m,s)$. Disjoint balls of radius $s/4$ around them show, by volume comparison, that there are at most $CM$ centers. Their balls of radius $s/2$ cover the set and are contained in the corresponding local balls $B(y,a_y)$, since $a_y\ge s$. Equation <a href="#m:eq:void-net-local-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-local-count">[m:eq:void-net-local-count]</a> bounds this contribution by $CMs^{-3}$.

For a distance dyad $u\le d(x)<2u$, choose a maximal $u/(2L)$-separated subset. Its centers lie in $\bigcup_m B(R_m,2u)$, so there are at most $CL^3M$ of them. Their local balls cover the dyad, because $a_y\ge d(y)/L\ge u/L$. Thus <a href="#m:eq:void-net-local-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-local-count">[m:eq:void-net-local-count]</a> bounds its expected count by $$CM\max\{(u/L)^{-3},1\}.$$ Take $u=2^ks$ for $0\le k\le K$, where $K$ is the least nonnegative integer such that $2^Ks\ge U$. These dyads cover $s\le d(x)<U$. The sum of the inverse-cube terms is bounded by $Cs^{-3}$. For the constant terms with $u<1$, use $1\le u^{-3}$; the number of remaining dyads, with $1\le u\le2U$, is bounded by a universal constant. Their total contribution is therefore at most $CMs^{-3}$. Adding <a href="#m:eq:void-net-far-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-net-far-count">[m:eq:void-net-far-count]</a> proves <a href="#m:eq:void-count" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:void-count">[m:eq:void-count]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="m:sec:barriers"></a>
### Deterministic comparison fields

We now construct positive fields that can be compared across all the local scales. The construction uses only the nuclear measure. Its output is a family of continuous offsets satisfying a nonlinear subsolution inequality, together with a strictly positive gap when the scale doubles. The gap will allow the quantum field to replace a comparison field in the next stage of the proof.

Fix a nuclear system and a small parameter $r_*>0$. Its value in terms of the excess charge, together with the dyadic scales used for the observations, will be chosen in Section <a href="#m:sec:conditioning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:sec:conditioning">8</a>. Every smallness threshold for $r_*$ in the present deterministic construction is universal and independent of the nuclear system.

Throughout this section, $p=3/2$, $k=((5/3)c_{\mathrm{TF}})^{-3/2}$, and $c_{\mathrm F}=4\pi k$. Recall that $K(x)=|x|^{-1}$ and that $a_x$ is the nuclear scale of Lemma <a href="#m:lem:screen-scale" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="m:lem:screen-scale">16</a>. All constants in this section are independent of the nuclear configuration and charges. They may depend on the fixed localization constants and the smooth radial function $g$ used for wave packets.

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Global Thomas–Fermi fields

The variational and comparison framework for point and smoothed sources is classical (Lieb and Simon 1977, Theorems II.14, II.17–II.18, and IV.7–IV.10) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[LiebSimon1977](#ref-LiebSimon1977)]. We give a proof on the Coulomb-space cone used here, including the decay needed for comparison on an unbounded domain. If $\zeta$ is a finite sum of positive point charges and nonnegative smooth compactly supported radial charges, write $V_\zeta=K*\zeta$. A radial charge is specified by its center, profile, and total mass. Set $$\mathcal X_+=\{\rho\ge0:\rho\in L^{5/3}(\mathbb R^3)
                  \cap(\dot H^1(\mathbb R^3))^*\}.$$ By Lemma <a href="#lem:loc-coulomb" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-coulomb">10</a>, the Coulomb energy of an element of this cone is the squared norm $D(\rho)=\|\nabla K*\rho\|_2^2/(8\pi)$.

<a id="stmt-m-lem-barrier-global-tf"></a>
<div id="m:lem:barrier-global-tf" class="lemma">

<a id="m:eq:global-tf-equation"></a>
**Lemma 31** (Global comparison problem). *Let $\zeta$ be as above, with total mass $Q>0$. The functional $$\mathcal T_\zeta(\rho)
   =c_{\mathrm{TF}}\int\rho^{5/3}-\int V_\zeta\rho+D(\rho),
       \qquad \rho\in\mathcal X_+,$$ has a unique minimizer. Its field $W=V_\zeta-K*\rho$ satisfies $$\label{m:eq:global-tf-equation}
 \rho=kW_+^p,\qquad
 \Delta W=-4\pi\zeta+c_{\mathrm F}W_+^p,\qquad
 W\ge0,\qquad \int\rho\le2Q.$$ The screening potential $K*\rho$ is continuous and tends to zero at infinity. Consequently $W$ tends to zero at infinity and is continuous away from point charges.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-m-lem-barrier-global-tf"><summary>Proof of stmt-m-lem-barrier-global-tf</summary>

*Proof.* Smear the point charges at any fixed positive widths and leave the smooth charges unchanged. This decomposes $V_\zeta=V_s+V_c$ with $V_s\in\dot H^1$ and compactly supported $V_c\in L^{5/2}$. Thus $$\left|\int V_s\rho\right|
