 |\nabla_y\mathcal K_z(y)|+|\nabla_z\mathcal K_z(y)|\le Ct^{-4}.$$ The center derivative is a weak derivative where $t_z$ has a corner; its stated bound also gives the needed Lipschitz estimate. For $r/2\le d_y\le8L_1r$ one has $a\asymp_{L_1}r$ and $$\label{a:eq:cond-tau}
 c_{L_1}a^w\le\tau\le Cs^w.$$ For example, when $d_y\ge r_0$, the formula is $\tau=c_1L\min(d_y,s)^w$; on the indicated band $\min(d_y,s)\ge c_{L_1}d_y$. The case $d_y<r_0$ is comparable, since $d_y\ge r_0/2$.

<a id="a:eq:cond-count-event"></a>
<a id="a:eq:cond-count-regularity"></a>
*An observed-count event.* Let $M_j$ be the number of entries of $Y_j$ with radii in $[r/4,12L_1r]$. For a sufficiently large fixed $C_{L_1}$, $$\label{a:eq:cond-count-event}
 \mathbb P\{M_j>C_{L_1}r^{-3}\}\le r^{40}.$$ Indeed, if the event had probability $p>r^{40}$, its tilted raw state from Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-tilt">26</a> would have offset $$\delta\le D_0+Cr^{-2.02}\log^5(e/r^{40}).$$ Each configuration compatible with the event has more than $C_{L_1}r^{-3}$ raw points in $[r/8,13L_1r]$, because observation displacements are at most $\sqrt3\ell_j=o(r)$. But <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a> bounds its expected count by $C'_{L_1}(r^{-3}+\sqrt{\delta r})\le2C'_{L_1}r^{-3}$ for small $s$, a contradiction if $C_{L_1}>2C'_{L_1}$. Call the complementary event in <a href="#a:eq:cond-count-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-count-event">[a:eq:cond-count-event]</a> $\mathcal C_j$. For data in $\mathcal C_j$, every compatible configuration has at most $C_{L_1}r^{-3}$ centers relevant to the master kernels on $r/2\le d_y\le8L_1r$. Conditional averaging and <a href="#a:eq:cond-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-kernel">[a:eq:cond-kernel]</a> imply there $$\label{a:eq:cond-count-regularity}
 \mu_j(y)\le Cr^{-6-3w},\qquad
 |\nabla\mu_j(y)|\le Cr^{-7-4w}.$$

<a id="a:eq:cond-positive-tail"></a>
<a id="a:eq:cond-cap"></a>
*A universal spatial field cap.* Fix $y$ in the same enlarged band and tilt an event $A'=\{H_j(y)>b\,d_y^{-4}\}$ of positive probability $p$. The original-law tower gives $$\mathbb E[H_j(y)\mid A']
  =V(y)-\mathbb E_{\Psi_{A'}}\sum_i(K*\mathcal K_{x_i})(y)
  \le\mathbb E_{\Psi_{A'}}J_y.$$ To justify the last inequality, a master smear whose potential at $y$ differs from the point potential has $|x_i-y|<t_{x_i}$ and hence $|x_i-y|\le2t_y<Aa_y$ for small $s$. All terms outside that exclusion therefore have their exact point potential, and dropping the remaining electron terms gives $J_y$. By <a href="#a:eq:screen-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-local">[a:eq:screen-local]</a> without an initial cut and Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-tilt">26</a>, $$\label{a:eq:cond-positive-tail}
 b\le C_2+C_{L_1}r^{2.49}\log^{5/2}(e/p).$$ Here $C_2$ is universal: the local field term in these units is $C d_y^4a_y^{-4}=CL^4$, and the contribution $C d_y^{7/2}\sqrt{D_0}$ is at most one after decreasing $s$. Solving <a href="#a:eq:cond-positive-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-positive-tail">[a:eq:cond-positive-tail]</a> for $p$ shows that for $b\ge2C_2$, $$\mathbb P\{d_y^4H_j(y)>b\}
   \le e\exp\{-c_{L_1}(b-C_2)^{2/5}r^{-0.996}\}.$$ Tail integration consequently gives, for each fixed $M>0$, $$\mathbb E(d_y^4H_j(y)-2C_2)_+\le C_{M,L_1}r^M.$$ Subharmonicity permits a simultaneous conclusion without a $Z$-dependent derivative estimate. Apply the ball submean inequality with radius $d_y/10$ for $3r/4\le d_y\le6L_1r$. The averaging ball is contained in $\{r/2\le d_z\le8L_1r\}$ and $d_z\asymp d_y$ there, so $$d_y^4H_j(y)\le C_3+Cr^{-3}
   \int_{\{r/2\le d_z\le8L_1r\}}
                (d_z^4H_j(z)-2C_2)_+\,\,\mathrm dz.$$ The constant $C_3$ is universal. The integral term has expectation $O_{M,L_1}(r^M)$, since its integration volume is $O_{L_1}(r^3)$. Markov’s inequality proves that, outside an event of probability $C_{M,L_1}r^M$, $$\label{a:eq:cond-cap}
 H_j(y)\le C_{\rm cap}d_y^{-4}
       \quad(3r/4\le d_y\le6L_1r),\qquad C_{\rm cap}=C_3+1.$$ This establishes the claimed order of dependence of the cap.

*A fixed spatial point and height.* Fix now $r\le d_y\le4L_1r$ and $h\in[h_l/2,2h_h]$. Consider either failure of <a href="#a:eq:cond-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-inverse">[a:eq:cond-inverse]</a> with tolerance $\xi/4$, intersected with $\mathcal C_j$. If its probability were at least $r^{42}$, call that event $A'$ and write $Q=\mathbb P(\,\cdot\mid A')$. The raw marginal is the state $\Psi_{A'}$ of Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-tilt">26</a>, with <a id="eq:obs-tilted-offset"></a>$$\label{eq:obs-tilted-offset}
 \delta\le D_0+Cr^{-2.02}\log^5(e/r^{42})
                    \le a^{-7+.02}$$ for small $s$. Apply the refined patch <a href="#a:eq:screen-refined" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-refined">[a:eq:screen-refined]</a> to this raw state at $y$, with $\beta=a^{.2}$ and $b=\beta a$. Extend $Q$ by fresh cut labels sampled conditional on the raw positions, exactly as in that patch. Let $X$ be these patch data, let $\rho_X^c$ be its conditional core density, and let $\rho,W,\sigma$ be its minimizing density, screened field, and fine-smear density. Thus $$W=V-K*\rho_X^c-K*\rho,\qquad
 \rho=kW_+^{3/2}\ \hbox{on the patch},\qquad
 \mathbb E_Q\left[D(\sigma-\rho)+\int W_-\sigma\right]
       \le Ca^{-7+.02}.$$ The roles of these densities and their laws are distinct. The pair $\mu_j,H_j$ keeps its original-observation definition in <a href="#a:eq:cond-master" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-master">[a:eq:cond-master]</a>. Under the event law $Q$, the fresh patch gives the conditional core density $\rho_X^c$ and its field $\Phi_X=V-K*\rho_X^c$; $\sigma$ is the fine smear of retained out particles, whereas $\rho$ is the auxiliary minimizing density and $W=\Phi_X-K*\rho$. The field comparison is made after taking $Q$-expectations in <a href="#a:eq:cond-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-transfer">[a:eq:cond-transfer]</a> below.

<a id="a:eq:cond-matching"></a>
Each compatible raw configuration matches the observed array $Y_j$ after a permutation, with displacement at most $\sqrt3\ell_j$ per position. Both configurations and their conditional averages at those same data therefore differ, when tested against $x\mapsto\mathcal K_x(y)$, by at most $Cr^{-3}\ell_jt^{-4}$. This follows from the count bound and the center Lipschitz estimate in <a href="#a:eq:cond-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-kernel">[a:eq:cond-kernel]</a>; only entries within $2t+O(\ell_j)$ of $y$ can contribute. All such raw particles are retained by the fresh patch. Its radial fine smear moves a retained point by at most $\sqrt3b$, adding at most $Cr^{-3}bt^{-4}$ to the error. Consequently, on every compatible path, including paths where the Coulomb error is large, $$\label{a:eq:cond-matching}
 |\mathcal R\sigma(y)-\mu_j(y)|
   \le Cr^{-3}(\ell_j+b)t^{-4}
   \le Ca^{-6}\big(a^{.01}\tau^{-4}+a^{.2}\tau^{-4}\big)
   =o(a^{-6}).$$ In the comparison with the conditional average, one may first compare each raw sum with $\sum_i\mathcal K_{Y_{j,i}}(y)$, then average this same bound using the original posterior law. The array is fixed in both comparisons, so no equality of posterior laws is being used.

<a id="a:eq:cond-smoothed-duality"></a>
Let $G=\{D(\sigma-\rho)\le a^{-7+.01}\}$. Then $Q(G^c)\le Ca^{.01}$. The test function $z\mapsto\mathcal K_z(y)$ has support in $B(y,2t)$ and gradient norm at most $Ct^{-5/2}$. Coulomb duality gives on $G$ $$\label{a:eq:cond-smoothed-duality}
 |\mathcal R(\sigma-\rho)(y)|
  \le Ca^{-7/2+.005}t^{-5/2}
  =Ca^{-6}a^{.005}\tau^{-5/2}=o(a^{-6}).$$ The positivity of the two powers used so far is explicit: $.01-4w=.00996$ and $.005-(5/2)w=.004975$.

<a id="a:eq:cond-patch-low"></a>
On $G$, a high-density antecedent forces <a id="a:eq:cond-patch-high"></a>$$\label{a:eq:cond-patch-high}
 W(y)\ge(h-\xi/16)d_y^{-4}.$$ For if the reverse strict inequality held, the oscillation bound <a href="#a:eq:screen-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-oscillation">[a:eq:screen-oscillation]</a> on $B(y,2t)$ would give $W(z)\le(h-\xi/32)d_y^{-4}$ there for sufficiently small $s$. This remains uniform when $W(y)$ is arbitrarily negative: the upper estimate is $W(y)+C\tau(a^{-4}+W(y)_-)$, and $v\mapsto v+C\tau v_-$ is increasing for $C\tau<1$. Using the patch equation and $\int\mathcal K_z(y)\,\,\mathrm dz=1+O(s^w)$ would then give $$\mathcal R\rho(y)
   \le k(h-\xi/32)^{3/2}d_y^{-6}(1+O(s^w))
   <kh^{3/2}d_y^{-6}-c_{h_l,h_h,\xi}a^{-6},$$ contradicting <a href="#a:eq:cond-matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-matching">[a:eq:cond-matching]</a>–<a href="#a:eq:cond-smoothed-duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-smoothed-duality">[a:eq:cond-smoothed-duality]</a>. Similarly, on $G$ a low-density antecedent forces $$\label{a:eq:cond-patch-low}
 W(y)\le(h+\xi/16)d_y^{-4}.$$ Indeed the contrary is positive, so the lower oscillation estimate gives $W(z)\ge(h+\xi/32)d_y^{-4}$ on the contributing ball; its reaction density contradicts the low antecedent with the same strict margin. Constants from $d_y=La$ are fixed before $s$ is decreased.

<a id="a:eq:cond-patch-means"></a>
*The negative field on the exceptional patch event.* The upper bound <a href="#a:eq:cond-patch-low" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-patch-low">[a:eq:cond-patch-low]</a> passes to expectation with an $o(a^{-4})$ error, by the patch bound $W(y)\le Ca^{-4}$ and $Q(G^c)\le Ca^{.01}$. To pass the lower bound to expectation we need more than this small probability. Under the high-density antecedent, <a href="#a:eq:cond-matching" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-matching">[a:eq:cond-matching]</a> holds on all paths and gives $\mathcal R\sigma(y)\ge ca^{-6}$, since $h\ge h_l/2>0$. The support and upper bound of the master kernel imply the deterministic mass bound $$M_\sigma:=\int_{B(y,2t)}\sigma\ge ca^{-6}t^3.$$ For $Y=W(y)_-$, <a href="#a:eq:screen-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-oscillation">[a:eq:screen-oscillation]</a> gives throughout this ball $W(z)_-\ge(1-C\tau)Y-C\tau a^{-4}$. Taking $C\tau\le1/2$, integrating against $\sigma$, and using the mass bound yields $$Y\le C(a^{-6}t^3)^{-1}\int W_-\sigma+C\tau a^{-4}.$$ Multiply by $\mathbf 1_{G^c}$ and use the refined patch budget under the same law $Q$. We obtain <a id="a:eq:cond-negative-exception"></a>$$\label{a:eq:cond-negative-exception}
 \mathbb E_Q[\mathbf 1_{G^c}W(y)_-]
  \le Ca^{-4}\big(a^{.02}\tau^{-3}+\tau\big)=o(a^{-4}),$$ because $.02-3w=.01997>0$. Thus the high and low antecedents give, respectively, $$\label{a:eq:cond-patch-means}
 \mathbb E_QW(y)\ge(h-\xi/16)d_y^{-4}-o(a^{-4}),\qquad
 \mathbb E_QW(y)\le(h+\xi/16)d_y^{-4}+o(a^{-4}).$$

<a id="a:eq:cond-transfer"></a>
*Transfer between the two conditioning procedures.* Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-transfer" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-transfer">27</a> with $V_s=V$, $V_c=0$ and $h_X=W$. Its two towers are taken under the original observation law and the fresh tilted patch law, respectively. The refined patch provides all its inputs: the raw density bound and Coulomb budget are <a href="#a:eq:screen-refined" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-refined">[a:eq:screen-refined]</a>, the deleted count bound is <a href="#m:eq:screen-deleted-shell" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="m:eq:screen-deleted-shell">[m:eq:screen-deleted-shell]</a>, and $\rho\le Ca^{-6}$ on the contributing ball by <a href="#a:eq:screen-interior" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-interior">[a:eq:screen-interior]</a>. Therefore $$\label{a:eq:cond-transfer}
 |\mathbb E_Q(H_j-W)(y)|
 \le Ca^{-4}\{\tau^{1/5}+\beta^{1/5}+\sqrt\beta
             +a^{.01}\tau^{-1/2}+\tau^{1/2}\}=o(a^{-4}).$$ Here $\beta=a^{.2}$ and <a href="#a:eq:cond-tau" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-tau">[a:eq:cond-tau]</a> makes every positive-power error vanish. The source-free potential remainder actually has the sharper power $\tau^2$, so the common upper bound is sufficient. No equality of posterior laws is used in this transfer.

On a high-failure event, its defining inequality is $d_y^4H_j(y)<h-\xi/4$, whereas <a href="#a:eq:cond-patch-means" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-patch-means">[a:eq:cond-patch-means]</a> and <a href="#a:eq:cond-transfer" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-transfer">[a:eq:cond-transfer]</a> give $d_y^4\mathbb E_QH_j(y)\ge h-\xi/16-o(1)$, a contradiction for small $s$. On a low-failure event the inequalities are reversed and give the same contradiction. Thus each such failure intersected with $\mathcal C_j$ has probability less than $r^{42}$. Including <a href="#a:eq:cond-count-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-count-event">[a:eq:cond-count-event]</a>, the two implications at a fixed point and height, with tolerance $\xi/4$, fail with probability at most $Cr^{40}$.

*Spatial and height nets.* Choose a net of the closed band $\{r\le d_y\le4L_1r\}$ with spacing at most $r^2$ and at most $C_{L_1}r^{-3}$ points. For example, select one band point in each intersecting cube of side $r^2/2$; the cube count follows from the volume of its $O(r^2)$ enlargement. Choose a finite height net in $[h_l/2,2h_h]$ with mesh at most $\xi/64$, including its endpoints. Exclude every point-and-height failure, the count failure, and the cap failure with $M=40$ in <a href="#a:eq:cond-cap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-cap">[a:eq:cond-cap]</a>. A union bound gives exceptional probability $Cr^{37}$, and hence at most $Cr^{25}$ for $r<1$.

<a id="a:eq:cond-field-oscillation"></a>
On the retained event, <a href="#a:eq:cond-count-regularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-count-regularity">[a:eq:cond-count-regularity]</a> shows that $d_y^6\mu_j(y)$ varies by at most $Cr^{1-4w}=o(1)$ between a band point and a net point within $Cr^2$. To control the field, work in a ball of radius $cr$ around a band point, contained in the enlarged cap band. On this ball $B$, let $Q=K*(\mathbf 1_B\mu_j)$. Then $H_j+Q$ is harmonic on $B$. The value and gradient of $Q$ are bounded by $Cr^{-4-3w}$ and $Cr^{-5-3w}$, respectively, so $H_j+Q$ is bounded above by $Cr^{-4-3w}$. Its nonnegative distance from that upper bound has, by the Poisson formula on a smaller concentric ball, oscillation bounded by its central value times the relative distance. It follows that $$\label{a:eq:cond-field-oscillation}
 |H_j(z)-H_j(y)|
   \le Cr\big(r^{-4-3w}+H_j(y)_-\big)
       \quad(|z-y|\le Cr^2).$$ This estimate also controls the change of the normalized field $d_y^4H_j(y)$, since $d_z/d_y=1+O(r)$. In particular, if its value at $y$ is below $h-\xi$, its value at $z$ is below $h-3\xi/4$ for small $s$. Arbitrarily negative values cause no problem: the relevant upper estimate has the form $v+Crv_-+O(r^{1-3w})$, increasing in $v$ when $Cr<1$. If the value at $y$ exceeds $h+\xi$, it is positive and the cap and <a href="#a:eq:cond-field-oscillation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-field-oscillation">[a:eq:cond-field-oscillation]</a> make its value at $z$ greater than $h+3\xi/4$.

Suppose now a high-density implication failed at some $(y,h)$. At a nearby spatial net point choose a height-net value $h'\in[h-\xi/4,h-\xi/8]$. The positive lower height bound and the $o(1)$ density variation preserve the high antecedent at $h'$, while the field is below $h-3\xi/4<h'-\xi/4$. This is an excluded net failure. For a low-density failure choose $h'\in[h+\xi/8,h+\xi/4]$; its antecedent is preserved and the field exceeds $h+3\xi/4>h'+\xi/4$. The height intervals lie inside $[h_l/2,2h_h]$ by the stipulated smallness of $\xi$. This proves simultaneity and finishes the proposition. ◻

</details>

We record also an unconditional estimate used to pay for a failed band. For fixed $L_1$ and $r\le d_y\le4L_1r$, the support and size of the master kernel, conditional Jensen, and <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a> in the original state give <a id="a:eq:cond-density-l2"></a>$$\label{a:eq:cond-density-l2}
 \|\mu_j(y)\|_2
  \le Ct_y^{-3}
    \left\|\#\{i:r/2<d_{x_i}<8L_1r\}\right\|_2
  \le Ct_y^{-3}r^{-3}\le Cr^{-6-3w}.$$ The middle inequality uses the offset $D_0$ and sufficiently small $s$; its constant is uniform in the state and scale.

The order of choices is relevant. The fixed screening constants, $g,c_1,w$, and the universal cap $C_{\rm cap}$ come first. One may then choose $L_1,h_l,h_h,\xi$ and a bounded offset $D_0$, and finally make $s$ small enough for every estimate above. Thereafter any sufficiently large $Z$ with $2r_0\le s$ is permitted. In particular no step requires $D_0=0$, and the number of dyadic scales introduces no loss in the constants.

<a id="a:sec:propagation"></a>
### Outward propagation and an electron deficit

The conditional comparisons let us extend a prescribed electron source from the scale $Z^{-1/3}$ to a small radius independent of $Z$. The result applies to ground states whose energy has a bounded offset from the minimum over all particle numbers.

<a id="stmt-a-prop-prop-deficit"></a>
<div id="a:prop:prop-deficit" class="proposition">

**Proposition 46** (Deficit inside a fixed small ball). *There is a universal constant $c_{\mathrm{def}}>0$ with the following property. For every $D_0\in[0,\infty)$ there is $s_*(D_0)>0$ such that, for each $0<s\le s_*(D_0)$, there is an integer $Z_*(D_0,s)$ for which every integer $Z\ge Z_*(D_0,s)$ and every normalized sector ground state $\Psi$ satisfying $$n\le3Z,\qquad q_n[\Psi]=E_n\le E+D_0$$ obey <a id="a:eq:prop-deficit"></a>$$\label{a:eq:prop-deficit}
 Z-\mathbb E_\Psi\#\{i:|x_i|<s/4\}
 \ge c_{\mathrm{def}}s^{-3}>1.$$ All thresholds are independent of the sector and of the choice of its ground state. In particular, admissible fixed radii $s$ can be taken arbitrarily small.*

</div>

To prove the deficit, we construct fields $u_j$ satisfying a lower bound for their Laplacian. Alongside the nuclear term $-4\pi\nu$, this bound uses the conditional density term $4\pi\mu_j$ inside radius $r_j$ and the model reaction $4\pi k(u_j)_+^{3/2}$ outside. Each outward step replaces that reaction by the actual density on one more annulus, then forgets the current observation array. An error density records the source omitted on exceptional data. A positive lower barrier and a small expected error will force a positive residual charge at the terminal scale. The construction also gives the following result at every intermediate stopping index.

<a id="stmt-prop-atomic-intermediate"></a>
<div id="prop:atomic-intermediate" class="proposition">

<a id="a:eq:prop-invariant"></a>
<a id="eq:atomic-prefix-error"></a>
**Proposition 47** (Intermediate atomic subsolutions). *There are universal constants $\varepsilon,B,C_{\rm inv}>0$ and $L_1>2$ with the following property. Fix $D_0<\infty$, then take $s>0$ sufficiently small and integer $Z$ sufficiently large as in Proposition <a href="#a:prop:prop-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:prop-deficit">46</a>. For every sector ground state in that proposition use the common observations and master packets <a href="#a:eq:cond-observations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-observations">[a:eq:cond-observations]</a>–<a href="#a:eq:cond-master" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-master">[a:eq:cond-master]</a>, with $r_0=\varepsilon Z^{-1/3}$ and $s/2<r_m\le s$. For each $0\le j\le m$ there exist jointly measurable $\mathcal F_j$-measurable fields $u_j$ and densities $p_j^{\rm err}$ with the following properties. Put $d=|y|$, $r=r_j$, and $f(t)=4\pi k(t_+)^{3/2}$, and define $$b_r(y)=Bd^{-4}\left(1-\frac r{8d}\right)\quad(d\ge r).$$ $$\label{a:eq:prop-invariant}
 \begin{split}
 \Delta u_j&\ge-4\pi\nu+4\pi\mathbf 1_{\{d<r\}}\mu_j
       -4\pi p_j^{\mathrm{err}}+\mathbf 1_{\{d\ge r\}}f(u_j),\\
 b_r\le u_j&\le C_{\mathrm{inv}}d^{-4}\quad(d\ge r),
 \qquad u_j=b_r\quad(d>L_1r).
 \end{split}$$ The differential inequality is global in distributions. The offsets $u_j-V$ are continuous and locally Lipschitz on all of $\mathbb R^3$; $p_j^{\rm err}\ge0$ is bounded and supported in $|y|\le r_j$. For each fixed system and step, the offsets have deterministic size and Lipschitz bounds on every compact set; the errors have deterministic size bounds. Universally, $$\label{eq:atomic-prefix-error}
 \mathbb E\int p_j^{\rm err}
 \le C r_0^{32}+C\sum_{i<j}r_i^{19/2-3w}
 \le C\left(r_0^{32}+r_j^{19/2-3w}\right),\qquad w=10^{-5}.$$ In particular this is at most $C(s^{32}+s^{19/2-3w})$. The expectations cannot in general be omitted for $j<m$; the terminal fields are deterministic. The same master kernels are used at every intermediate stopping index. The constants $\varepsilon,B,C_{\rm inv},L_1$ are independent of any additional inverse-comparison parameters. Such a comparison uses these same observations whenever its own smallness condition on $s$ is met.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-atomic-intermediate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-atomic-intermediate</summary>

*Proof of Propositions <a href="#prop:atomic-intermediate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:atomic-intermediate">47</a> and <a href="#a:prop:prop-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:prop-deficit">46</a>.* Use the observations and master packets <a href="#a:eq:cond-observations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-observations">[a:eq:cond-observations]</a>–<a href="#a:eq:cond-master" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-master">[a:eq:cond-master]</a>, with $\varepsilon$ fixed during initialization below and $Z$ large enough that $m\ge1$. The resulting densities and fields satisfy <a id="a:eq:prop-master-recall"></a>$$\label{a:eq:prop-master-recall}
 \mathbb E[\mu_j\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]=\mu_{j+1},\qquad
 \mathbb E[H_j\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]=H_{j+1}.$$

<a id="a:eq:prop-barrier"></a>
<a id="a:eq:prop-gap"></a>
Write $\kappa=1/8$. The barrier $b_r$ in the proposition is positive on $d\ge r$ and, for sufficiently small fixed $B>0$, satisfies $$\label{a:eq:prop-barrier}
 \Delta b_r=Bd^{-6}(12-20\kappa r/d)
 \ge\frac{19}{2}Bd^{-6}\ge f(b_r).$$ For example, $4\pi k\sqrt B\le19/2$ suffices. If $R=2r$, then $$\label{a:eq:prop-gap}
 b_r-b_R=B\kappa r d^{-5}\ge\gamma R^{-4}
 \quad(R\le d\le1.1R),\qquad
 \gamma=\frac{B\kappa}{2(1.1)^5}>0.$$

<a id="a:eq:prop-init-good"></a>
*Initialization.* With probability at least $1-Cr_0^{35}$, the initial conditional density satisfies $$\label{a:eq:prop-init-good}
 P_0(y):=K*(\mathbf 1_{\{d<r_0\}}\mu_0)(y)
 \le0.1\,Z/r_0\qquad(r_0\le |y|\le1.1r_0).$$ To prove this, fix a point $y$ in this annulus and let $A_y=\{P_0(y)>0.05Z/r_0\}$, an event of $\mathcal F_0$. Suppose that $p=\mathbb P(A_y)>r_0^{40}$. By Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-tilt">26</a>, the raw marginal conditioned on this event is the squared law of an antisymmetric state of offset at most $$D_0+C r_0^{-2.02}\log^5(e/r_0^{40}).$$ For the fixed $\varepsilon$ this is at most $Z^{7/3}$ when $Z$ is large enough. The global estimate <a href="#eq:atomic-global-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:atomic-global-density">[eq:atomic-global-density]</a> therefore bounds the tilted density $\rho_{A_y}$ by $\int\rho_{A_y}^{5/3}\le C Z^{7/3}$, with a universal $C$.

Only packet centers with $|x|<2r_0$ can contribute to the truncated density in $P_0$: a center outside this ball has $t_x\le c_1s^w|x|<|x|/2$, so its packet misses $B(0,r_0)$. A truncated packet has potential at most that of the full packet, which by radial smearing is at most $K(y-x)$. The conditional tower identity and Hölder’s inequality consequently give $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E[P_0(y)\mid A_y]
 &\le\int_{|x|<2r_0}\frac{\rho_{A_y}(x)}{|y-x|}\,\,\mathrm dx\\
 &\le C Z^{7/5}r_0^{1/5}
   =C\varepsilon^{6/5}\,Z/r_0.
\end{aligned}$$ Choose $\varepsilon$ small enough that the last coefficient is less than $0.05$. This contradicts the definition of $A_y$, so $\mathbb P(A_y)\le r_0^{40}$.

The minimum packet width is $t_{\min}=c_1r_0^{1+w}$. Integrating $|y-z|^{-2}$ against a bounded packet gives the deterministic bound $$\|\nabla P_0\|_\infty\le Cn t_{\min}^{-2}
 \le C Z r_0^{-2-2w}.$$ A net in the annulus of spacing $O(r_0^2)$ has $O(r_0^{-3})$ points. The union bound gives failure probability $O(r_0^{37})$, and the variation between a point and its net point, divided by $Z/r_0$, is $O(r_0^{1-2w})=o(1)$. For large $Z$ this proves <a href="#a:eq:prop-init-good" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-init-good">[a:eq:prop-init-good]</a>, with the stated weaker exponent $35$.

<a id="a:eq:prop-initial-error"></a>
Let $G_0$ denote the event in <a href="#a:eq:prop-init-good" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-init-good">[a:eq:prop-init-good]</a>, and set $$\mu^{\mathrm{in}}=\mathbf 1_{G_0}\mathbf 1_{\{d<r_0\}}\mu_0,
 \qquad p_0^{\mathrm{err}}=\mathbf 1_{G_0^c}\mathbf 1_{\{d<r_0\}}\mu_0.$$ On $d<2r_0$ use the branch $$v=V-K*\mu^{\mathrm{in}}-0.7Z/r_0
            +C_3(Z/r_0)^{3/2}d^2.$$ Choose $C_3$ universally so that $6C_3\ge4\pi k2^{3/2}$, and then choose $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small to meet the preceding tilt condition and $4C_3\varepsilon^{3/2}\le0.05$. The quadratic term on this ball is at most $0.05Z/r_0$, since $Zr_0^3=\varepsilon^3$. For $r_0\le d<2r_0$ we thus have $v\le2Z/r_0$ and $$\Delta v=-4\pi\nu+4\pi\mu^{\mathrm{in}}
                +6C_3(Z/r_0)^{3/2},
 \qquad 6C_3(Z/r_0)^{3/2}\ge f(v).$$ On $r_0\le d<1.06r_0$, the good-event bound (and the easier bound $\mu^{\mathrm{in}}=0$ on its complement) give $$v\ge(1/1.06-0.1-0.7)Z/r_0>0.14Z/r_0.$$ On $1.9r_0<d<2r_0$ we instead have $v\le(1/1.9-0.7+0.05)Z/r_0<0$. Fix $B$ satisfying the barrier condition and $0<B\le0.1\varepsilon^3$. Then $v>b_{r_0}$ on the lower overlap, whereas $v<0<b_{r_0}$ on the upper overlap. Define $$u_0=\begin{cases}
 v,&d<1.06r_0,\\
 \max(v,b_{r_0}),&r_0<d<2r_0,\\
 b_{r_0},&d>1.9r_0.
 \end{cases}$$ The definitions agree on their open overlaps. Lemma <a href="#lem:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:maximum">28</a> proves <a href="#a:eq:prop-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-invariant">[a:eq:prop-invariant]</a> initially; near $d=r_0$ the branch $v$ alone attains the maximum, and its displayed Laplacian supplies both required densities. The exterior upper bound holds, for example, when $C_{\mathrm{inv}}\ge\max(B,32\varepsilon^3)$. Also, using $n\le3Z=3\varepsilon^3r_0^{-3}$, $$\label{a:eq:prop-initial-error}
 \mathbb E\int p_0^{\mathrm{err}}
 \le n\mathbb P(G_0^c)\le C r_0^{32}.$$

<a id="a:eq:prop-choices"></a>
*Order of the remaining constants.* The constants $C_3,\varepsilon,B$ have now been fixed universally. Increase $C_{\mathrm{inv}}$ to exceed also the universal cap $C_{\mathrm{cap}}$ in Proposition <a href="#a:prop:cond-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:cond-comparison">45</a>. After fixing $\gamma$ in <a href="#a:eq:prop-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-gap">[a:eq:prop-gap]</a>, choose $0<\lambda_2<\lambda_1<\gamma/2$. Choose $L_1>2$ so large that $C_{\mathrm{inv}}L_1^{-4}<\lambda_2$. This ensures $$C_{\mathrm{inv}}d^{-4}-\lambda_2R^{-4}<b_R
 \qquad(d>L_1R).$$ Next fix $0<h_l<B/4$ and $h_h>C_{\mathrm{inv}}$, and then choose $e_0,\xi>0$ sufficiently small that $$\label{a:eq:prop-choices}
 2e_0(2L_1)^4<B/4,\qquad
 16\xi+2e_0<\lambda_1-\lambda_2,\qquad \xi<\lambda_2,$$ with $\xi$ also in the range of Proposition <a href="#a:prop:cond-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:cond-comparison">45</a>. Only now restrict $s$ according to that proposition, $D_0$, and the other smallness conditions in this proof. Finally take $Z$ large according to $D_0,s$ and the initialization requirements. Thus no constant is chosen in terms of a later scale or a particular state.

<a id="a:eq:prop-branches"></a>
<a id="a:eq:prop-step-error"></a>
*One outward step.* Suppose that <a href="#a:eq:prop-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-invariant">[a:eq:prop-invariant]</a> holds at $r=r_j$, write $R=2r$, and abbreviate $u=u_j$. On the good band event of Proposition <a href="#a:prop:cond-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:cond-comparison">45</a>, for the band $r\le d\le4L_1r=2L_1R$, introduce $$v_1=u-\lambda_1R^{-4},\qquad
 v_2=H_j-\lambda_2R^{-4}.$$ Define a new function on a fixed open cover by $$\label{a:eq:prop-branches}
 \begin{array}{c|c}
 \text{region}&u'\\ \hline
 d<1.08R&\max(v_1,v_2)\\
 1.04R<d<2L_1R&\max(v_1,v_2,b_R)\\
 d>L_1R&b_R.
 \end{array}$$ On bad band data omit $v_2$ in every row. In both cases set $$\label{a:eq:prop-step-error}
 \widetilde p=p_j^{\mathrm{err}}
           +\mathbf 1_{\{\mathrm{bad}\}}\mathbf 1_{\{r\le d<R\}}\mu_j.$$ On the lower overlap, <a href="#a:eq:prop-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-gap">[a:eq:prop-gap]</a> gives $v_1\ge b_r-\lambda_1R^{-4}>b_R$. On the upper overlap, the caps on $u$ and, on good data, $H_j$ put both shifted branches below $b_R$. The definitions therefore agree. For $R\le d<1.08R$ the lower bound $u'\ge b_R$ follows already from $v_1$; farther out the barrier is included explicitly. The upper bound $u'\le C_{\mathrm{inv}}d^{-4}$ and the equality $u'=b_R$ for $d>L_1R$ follow from the same caps.

<a id="a:eq:prop-near-active"></a>
We prove the distributional inequality at radius $R$ before averaging. The open-neighborhood formulation is as follows. At a point $y_0$ in one of the open sets of <a href="#a:eq:prop-branches" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-branches">[a:eq:prop-branches]</a>, let $M$ be the maximum of its candidate family and put $\eta=e_0R^{-4}$. Keep precisely those candidates $v$ with $M(y_0)-v(y_0)\le\eta$, and choose a maximizer $v_*$ at $y_0$. Continuity and finiteness give an open neighborhood $U$ of $y_0$, inside the chosen open set, on which every discarded candidate is strictly below $v_*$ and every retained candidate satisfies $$\label{a:eq:prop-near-active}
 M-v<2e_0R^{-4}.$$ The retained family has maximum $M$ throughout $U$. At the origin make this construction with the continuous offsets by $V$; the family there contains only branches having that common singularity.

<a id="a:eq:prop-common"></a>
Each retained branch satisfies on the whole of this neighborhood the common inequality $$\label{a:eq:prop-common}
 \Delta v\ge-4\pi\nu+4\pi\mathbf 1_{\{d<R\}}\mu_j
          -4\pi\widetilde p+\mathbf 1_{\{d\ge R\}}f(v).$$ For $v_1$, retain its old distributional inequality and compare its right side with the new one almost everywhere. On $d<r$ the old source suffices. On $d\ge R$, use $f(u)\ge f(v_1)$. On the gain region $r\le d<R$, bad data are paid by the added error in <a href="#a:eq:prop-step-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-step-error">[a:eq:prop-step-error]</a>. On good data, $v_2$ is a candidate there, so <a href="#a:eq:prop-near-active" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-near-active">[a:eq:prop-near-active]</a> gives $$H_j\le u-(\lambda_1-\lambda_2-2e_0)R^{-4}.$$ Moreover $h_l<7B/8\le d^4u\le C_{\mathrm{inv}}<h_h$. If $4\pi\mu_j>f(u)$, the high implication in <a href="#a:eq:cond-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-inverse">[a:eq:cond-inverse]</a>, at height $h=d^4u$, would give $$H_j\ge u-\xi d^{-4}\ge u-16\xi R^{-4},$$ contrary to <a href="#a:eq:prop-choices" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-choices">[a:eq:prop-choices]</a>. Hence $4\pi\mu_j\le f(u)$, and the old reaction supplies the added source.

For $v_2$, which occurs only on good data, start from its identity on all of $U$, $$\Delta v_2=-4\pi\nu+4\pi\mu_j.$$ This supplies <a href="#a:eq:prop-common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-common">[a:eq:prop-common]</a> on $d<R$, since $\widetilde p\ge0$. At any remaining point where $v_2$ is retained, the lower splice or the included barrier gives $M\ge b_R$, and $d\le2L_1R$. Thus $$d^4v_2\ge B(1-\kappa R/d)-2e_0(d/R)^4
 \ge7B/8-2e_0(2L_1)^4>5B/8>h_l.$$ The cap also gives $d^4v_2\le C_{\mathrm{cap}}<h_h$. If $4\pi\mu_j<f(v_2)$, the low implication in <a href="#a:eq:cond-inverse" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-inverse">[a:eq:cond-inverse]</a>, with $h=d^4v_2$, would imply $$H_j\le v_2+\xi d^{-4}
       \le v_2+\xi R^{-4}<v_2+\lambda_2R^{-4}=H_j,$$ which is impossible. Consequently $4\pi\mu_j\ge f(v_2)$ there. The barrier branch occurs only where $d>R$ and satisfies the common inequality by <a href="#a:eq:prop-barrier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-barrier">[a:eq:prop-barrier]</a>.

These verifications compare zero-order densities almost everywhere on the same open set $U$; they do not restrict or differentiate a weak inequality across an activity set or a sphere. In particular they apply on a neighborhood crossing $d=R$. The common reaction is $F(y,t)=\mathbf 1_{\{d\ge R\}}f(t)$, which meets Lemma <a href="#lem:maximum" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:maximum">28</a>’s hypotheses after canceling $V$ near the origin. That lemma proves <a href="#a:eq:prop-common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-common">[a:eq:prop-common]</a> for the local maximum. These local conclusions give the inequality globally by a partition of unity. We have proved the desired invariant at radius $R$ with density $\mu_j$, function $u'$, and error $\widetilde p$.

*Forgetting the current observations.* Define the next offset and error by conditional integration, $$u_{j+1}=V+\mathbb E[u'-V\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}],\qquad
 p_{j+1}^{\mathrm{err}}=\mathbb E[\widetilde p\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}].$$ We give the regularity justification as well as the formal calculation. For the fixed system $t_{\min}>0$, and integration of each packet gives deterministic bounds $$\|\mu_j\|_\infty\le Cnt_{\min}^{-3},\qquad
 \|K*\mu_j\|_\infty\le Cnt_{\min}^{-1},\qquad
 \|\nabla K*\mu_j\|_\infty\le Cnt_{\min}^{-2}.$$ The same potential bounds hold for the initially truncated density. The explicit initial branches therefore have deterministic local Lipschitz bounds for their offsets. Subtracting a constant and taking a finite maximum preserve such bounds. Where $b_R$ occurs, its offset $b_R-V$ is smooth and separated from the origin. The fixed open cover and agreement on overlaps preserve local bounds after gluing. The errors are bounded, nonnegative, and supported in a deterministic ball. Induction supplies all the claimed bounds at every step, without requiring them to be uniform in $Z$.

All functions are jointly measurable in data and position. Indeed the conditional densities have the likelihood versions described in Section <a href="#a:sec:conditioning" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:sec:conditioning">10</a>; the initialization flag is a supremum of a continuous function on a fixed compact annulus, testable on a countable dense set; and the band flags and finite maxima are measurable. Conditional integration of functions with deterministic local Lipschitz bounds preserves those bounds. It also commutes with weak derivatives by Fubini. Nonnegative smooth tests in a countable dense family first give simultaneous almost-sure weak inequalities; local bounds extend them to every test. Thus the following conditional integration is legitimate as an inequality of distributions.

Integrate <a href="#a:eq:prop-common" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-common">[a:eq:prop-common]</a> conditionally on $\mathcal F_{j+1}$. The tower property supplies $\mu_{j+1}$, while convexity gives $$\mathbb E[f(u')\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]
       \ge f\bigl(V+\mathbb E[u'-V\mid\mathcal F_{j+1}]\bigr).$$ The deterministic bounds $b_R\le u'\le C_{\mathrm{inv}}d^{-4}$ and the equality outside $L_1R$ pass to this average. Positivity, boundedness, and support of the error do as well. This proves <a href="#a:eq:prop-invariant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-invariant">[a:eq:prop-invariant]</a> at step $j+1$.

<a id="a:eq:prop-total-error"></a>
*Summing the lost source.* For $r\le d_y<2r$, every master packet contributing to $\mu_j(y)$ has its center in a fixed annulus comparable to $r$, and its width is comparable to $t_y$. Conditional Jensen and the annular count estimate <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a>, applied with offset at most $D_0$, yield $$\|\mu_j(y)\|_2\le Ct_y^{-3}r^{-3}
              \le Cr^{-6-3w}.$$ Here $r\le s\le1$ and $s$ is small according to $D_0$, so $\max(r^{-3},1)+\sqrt{D_0r}\le Cr^{-3}$. The bad band probability is at most $Cr^{25}$ by Proposition <a href="#a:prop:cond-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:cond-comparison">45</a>. Cauchy–Schwarz in data and integration over the gain annulus give $$\mathbb E\int(\widetilde p-p_j^{\mathrm{err}})
 \le C r^{25/2}r^3r^{-6-3w}
 =Cr^{\alpha},\qquad \alpha=\frac{19}{2}-3w>0.$$ Conditional averaging preserves the expected integral. Stopping the sum before any index $j$ and using <a href="#a:eq:prop-initial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-initial-error">[a:eq:prop-initial-error]</a> proves <a href="#eq:atomic-prefix-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:atomic-prefix-error">[eq:atomic-prefix-error]</a>, with the same master kernels and without replacing an expectation by a pathwise bound. At the terminal index the geometric progression of radii gives $$\label{a:eq:prop-total-error}
 \int p_m^{\mathrm{err}}
 =\mathbb E\int p_m^{\mathrm{err}}
 \le Cr_0^{32}+C\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}r_j^\alpha
 \le C(s^{32}+s^\alpha).$$ The last error is deterministic because $\mathcal F_m$ is trivial. The constants are independent of the number of scales and of $Z$.

*From the potential to the raw electron count.* Put $r=r_m$, $u=u_m$, and introduce the compactly supported signed measure $$S=\nu-\mathbf 1_{\{d<r\}}\mu_m\,\,\mathrm dy+p_m^{\mathrm{err}}\,\,\mathrm dy.$$ The invariant implies $\Delta(u-K*S)\ge0$. Its singularities cancel in the identity $$u-K*S=(u-V)+K*(\mathbf 1_{\{d<r\}}\mu_m)-K*p_m^{\mathrm{err}},$$ whose right side is continuous and locally $H^1$ on all of $\mathbb R^3$. The difference tends to zero at infinity: $u=b_r$ outside $L_1r$, and the potential of the finite compact source $S$ tends to zero. For each $a>0$, the positive part $(u-K*S-a)_+$ has compact support and is an admissible $H^1$ test in its subharmonic inequality. Testing gives a nonpositive squared gradient integral, hence this positive part vanishes. Letting $a\downarrow0$ proves $u\le K*S$.

<a id="a:eq:prop-charge"></a>
On the sphere $d=2r$, the invariant gives $u\ge b_r(2r)=15B r^{-4}/256$. The spherical mean of $K*S$ there equals the total mass of $S$ divided by $2r$, since $S$ is supported in $\{d\le r\}$. Consequently $$\label{a:eq:prop-charge}
 Z-\int_{d<r_m}\mu_m+\int p_m^{\mathrm{err}}
 \ge\frac{15B}{128}r_m^{-3}.$$ No sign condition on the measure $S$ is needed for this mean identity.

Finally $\mu_m$ is the unconditional master-smoothed density. If $|x|<s/4$, then $r_0\le s/2$ and $D_x^0\le s/2$, so $t_x\le c_1(s/2)s^w<s/4$. The packet at $x$ is therefore wholly contained in $B(0,s/2)\subset B(0,r_m)$. Since every packet has mass one, $$\mathbb E_\Psi\#\{i:|x_i|<s/4\}\le\int_{d<r_m}\mu_m.$$ Combining this with <a href="#a:eq:prop-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-charge">[a:eq:prop-charge]</a>, <a href="#a:eq:prop-total-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:prop-total-error">[a:eq:prop-total-error]</a>, and $r_m\le s$ gives $$Z-\mathbb E_\Psi\#\{i:|x_i|<s/4\}
 \ge\frac{15B}{128}s^{-3}-C(s^{32}+s^\alpha).$$ Take $c_{\mathrm{def}}=15B/256$ and decrease $s_*(D_0)$ so that the error is at most $c_{\mathrm{def}}s^{-3}$ and $c_{\mathrm{def}}s^{-3}>1$ for every $0<s\le s_*(D_0)$. All remaining requirements are met by increasing $Z_*(D_0,s)$. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="a:sec:neutral"></a>
### Returning to the neutral sector

The deficit estimate was proved for ground states whose energy is within a fixed distance of the minimum over all particle numbers. We first apply it at that minimum. A screened deletion comparison will then put the neutral and singly ionized ground states in the same class.

<a id="a:eq:tail-inner-field"></a>
We record explicitly the harmonic-field estimate used in the deletion and again in the final tail argument. For a configuration $\omega=(x_i,\sigma_i)_{i=1}^n$ and $h>0$, write $$\label{a:eq:tail-inner-field}
 B_h(x;\omega)=V(x)-\sum_{|x_i|<h}K(x-x_i).$$ This field is harmonic on $\{|x|>h\}$, configuration by configuration.

<a id="stmt-a-lem-tail-harmonic"></a>
<div id="a:lem:tail-harmonic" class="lemma">

<a id="a:eq:tail-harmonic-bound"></a>
**Lemma 48** (Conditional fields on annuli). *Fix positive constants $\alpha<\alpha'<\beta'<\beta$ and $\alpha_0<\beta_0$. Let $u>0$, $0<h\le\alpha u/2$, and let $\psi$ be a normalized $\delta$-state with $n\le3Z$, where $\delta<\infty$ is arbitrary. Let $Y$ be the data from a cut whose out support lies in $\{\alpha_0u<|x|<\beta_0u\}$ and whose squared gradient cost is at most $C u^{-2}$. The cut may also be absent, in which case $Y$ is trivial. There is a jointly measurable version $$H_Y(x)=\mathbb E_\psi[B_h(x)\mid Y]$$ which is harmonic on $\{\alpha u<|x|<\beta u\}$ for almost every datum. Put $$M(Y)=\sup_{\alpha'u\le|x|\le\beta'u}(H_Y(x))_+.$$ Then $$\label{a:eq:tail-harmonic-bound}
 \|M\|_2\le C\left\{
 \frac{\max(u^{-3},1)+\sqrt{\delta u}}{u}
 +\sum_{j=0}^\infty
 \frac{\max((2^jh)^{-3},1)+\sqrt{\delta\,2^jh}}
      {\max(u,2^jh)}\right\}.$$ The constant depends only on the fixed annular ratios and cut-gradient constant, in addition to the fixed screening constants $L,A$. In particular it does not depend on $n,Z,\delta,h,u$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-lem-tail-harmonic"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-lem-tail-harmonic</summary>

<a id="a:eq:tail-addback"></a>
*Proof.* For $x$ in the broad annulus, every source with $|x_i|<h$ lies outside $B(x,Aa_x)$: indeed $h\le|x|/2$ and $A/L<1/2$. Consequently $$\label{a:eq:tail-addback}
 B_h(x)=J_x+
 \sum_{\substack{|x_i|\ge h\\|x_i-x|\ge Aa_x}}K(x-x_i).$$ If $2^jh\le|x_i|<2^{j+1}h$ and the summand occurs, then $$K(x-x_i)\le\frac{C}{\max(u,2^jh)}.$$ For radii comparable to or below $u$ this follows from the exclusion $|x_i-x|\ge Aa_x\ge A\alpha u/L$; for radii greater than $2\beta u$ it follows from $|x_i-x|\ge|x_i|/2$.

The initial-cut hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#a:prop:screen-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:screen-local">20</a> hold uniformly here. Cover its support by a fixed finite number of cells $B_z$ with $a_z\asymp u$, the constants being allowed to depend on $L$ and the annular ratios. For each target $x$ in the broad annulus these cell scales are comparable to $a_x$. The gradient bound can be written as $\sum_zD_z^2a_z^{-2}\mathbf 1_{B_z}$, with $D_z^2\le C(a_z/u)^2$. Since $a_zm_z\ge1$ and the screening parameter $P\ge1$, $$\frac{D_z^2}{a_zm_z}\le C\le C_0\sqrt P.$$ Enlarging the fixed cover constant $C_0$ suffices for every finite $\delta$. Thus <a href="#a:eq:screen-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-local">[a:eq:screen-local]</a>, conditional Jensen, the triangle inequality in $L^2$, and <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a> applied to <a href="#a:eq:tail-addback" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-addback">[a:eq:tail-addback]</a> give, pointwise in the spatial variable, $$\|(H_Y(x))_+\|_2\le C\left\{
 \frac{\max(u^{-3},1)+\sqrt{\delta u}}{u}
 +\sum_{j\ge0}
 \frac{\max((2^jh)^{-3},1)+\sqrt{\delta\,2^jh}}
      {\max(u,2^jh)}\right\}.$$ The series converges: its large-$j$ terms are bounded by a constant times $2^{-j}h^{-1}+\sqrt\delta\,2^{-j/2}h^{-1/2}$.

For completeness, conditional fields here have simultaneous spatial versions. On every compact subset of $\{|x|>h\}$, the kernels in <a href="#a:eq:tail-inner-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-inner-field">[a:eq:tail-inner-field]</a> and each fixed number of their derivatives have deterministic bounds for the fixed finite system. Conditional integration therefore gives continuous harmonic functions, with measurable dependence on the data. One may first use the conditional law of configurations supplied by the cut and then differentiate under that integral. For a harmonic function $H$, its positive part is subharmonic. Balls of radius $c u$ about points of the smaller annulus remain in the broad annulus, so their mean-value inequalities imply $$\sup_{\alpha'u\le|x|\le\beta'u}H(x)_+
 \le C u^{-3}\int_{\alpha u<|z|<\beta u}H(z)_+\,\,\mathrm dz.$$ Minkowski’s integral inequality and the pointwise bound prove <a href="#a:eq:tail-harmonic-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-harmonic-bound">[a:eq:tail-harmonic-bound]</a>. Suprema can be taken on countable dense sets, which also proves their measurability. ◻

</details>

<a id="a:eq:tail-deletion-field"></a>
<a id="a:eq:tail-distant-field"></a>
Two forms of this estimate will be used. With fixed $t>0$, $h=t/4$, $u=2^\ell t$, and $\delta=0$, it gives for the positive conditional supremum on $u\le|x|\le2u$ the bound $$\label{a:eq:tail-deletion-field}
 \|M\|_2\le C_t\frac{1+\ell}{u}.$$ Indeed the terms with $2^jh\le u$ have total numerator at most $C_t(1+\ell)$, and the remaining series is $O_t(u^{-1})$. With $h=u/8$, $u\ge1$, and the smaller annulus $u/2\le|x|\le4u$, the broad annulus $u/4<|x|<6u$ is admissible and gives $$\label{a:eq:tail-distant-field}
 \|M\|_2\le C\big(u^{-1}+\sqrt\delta\,u^{-1/2}\big).$$ In the first application the broad annulus can be $u/2<|x|<3u$. The non-strict equality $h=\alpha u/2$ causes no boundary issue, since the broad annulus is open and still separated from the sources.

<a id="stmt-a-lem-tail-deficit-upper"></a>
<div id="a:lem:tail-deficit-upper" class="lemma">

**Lemma 49** (An upper bound on an inner deficit). *For each fixed $t>0$ and every normalized $\delta$-state with $n\le3Z$, <a id="a:eq:tail-deficit-upper"></a>$$\label{a:eq:tail-deficit-upper}
 Z-\mathbb E\#\{i:|x_i|<t/4\}\le C_t(1+\sqrt\delta).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-lem-tail-deficit-upper"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-lem-tail-deficit-upper</summary>

*Proof.* Use Lemma <a href="#a:lem:tail-harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:lem:tail-harmonic">48</a> with no cut, $h=t/4$, and $u=t$. Its convergent series is bounded by $C_t(1+\sqrt\delta)$. The spherical average of the expected field $B_{t/4}$ on $|x|=t$ equals $$\frac{Z-\mathbb E\#\{i:|x_i|<t/4\}}{t},$$ by the spherical mean of the Coulomb kernel. Bounding this average from above by the positive supremum proves the assertion, with the fixed factor $t$ absorbed into $C_t$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-a-prop-tail-offset"></a>
<div id="a:prop:tail-offset" class="proposition">

<a id="a:eq:tail-offset"></a>
**Proposition 50** (A bounded offset for neutral sectors). *There are universal constants $C,Z_0<\infty$ such that, for $Z\ge Z_0$, $$\label{a:eq:tail-offset}
 E_{Z-1}\le E+C.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-prop-tail-offset"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-prop-tail-offset</summary>

*Proof.* Take a normalized ground state $\Psi_*$ in sector $N_*$, where $Z\le N_*\le3Z$ and $E_{N_*}=E$, as provided by Lemma <a href="#lem:atomic-sector" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:atomic-sector">9</a>. Proposition <a href="#a:prop:prop-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:prop-deficit">46</a>, with $D_0=0$, supplies a fixed sufficiently small $t>0$ such that $$\mathbb E_{\Psi_*}\big[Z-\#\{i:|x_i|<2t\}\big]>1$$ for all sufficiently large $Z$. Choose a smooth square partition $c^2+o^2=1$, with $c=1$ on $|x|\le t$, $c=0$ on $|x|\ge2t$, and $|\nabla c|^2+|\nabla o|^2\le C t^{-2}$ supported in the intervening annulus. Let $X$ be its full out data and set $q^c=Z-n_c$, where $n_c$ is the number of core particles. Since $n_c\le\#\{i:|x_i|<2t\}$, we have $\mathbb Eq^c>1$.

We first show that the favorable event $G=\{n_c\le Z-1\}$ has probability bounded below independently of $Z$. Independently of the cut labels, observe one unordered array with the smooth compact noise law of Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-tilt">26</a>, choosing its fixed width so that each spatial displacement is less than $t/8$. Put $$q^{\rm obs}=Z-\#\{\text{array entries in }B(0,t/2)\}.$$ Every entry counted here comes from a particle of radius less than $5t/8$, which must be core. Thus $q^c\le q^{\rm obs}$ on the enlarged probability space. Conversely every particle of radius less than $t/4$ produces an entry in $B(0,t/2)$.

For an event $A_\lambda=\{q^{\rm obs}>\lambda\}$ of probability $p>0$, the single-array version of <a href="#eq:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:obs-tilt">[eq:obs-tilt]</a> produces a normalized state $\Psi_{A_\lambda}$ whose raw configuration law is the law conditioned on that event and whose offset satisfies $$\delta\le C_t\log^5(e/p).$$ Every compatible configuration obeys $Z-\#\{|x_i|<t/4\}>\lambda$. Hence Lemma <a href="#a:lem:tail-deficit-upper" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:lem:tail-deficit-upper">49</a> gives $$\lambda\le C_t\big(1+\log^{5/2}(e/p)\big).$$ For sufficiently large $\lambda$ this implies $p\le e\exp(-c_t\lambda^{2/5})$; the inequality is also harmless when $p=0$. Integrating the tail yields <a id="a:eq:tail-favorable-probability"></a>$$\label{a:eq:tail-favorable-probability}
 \mathbb E(q^c)_+^2\le\mathbb E(q^{\rm obs})_+^2\le C_t,
 \qquad
 \mathbb P(G)=\mathbb P(q^c>0)
 \ge\frac{(\mathbb E(q^c)_+)^2}{\mathbb E(q^c)_+^2}\ge c_t>0.$$ Here the equality of events uses the integer-valued core count, and $\mathbb E(q^c)_+\ge\mathbb Eq^c>1$.

<a id="a:eq:tail-deletion-attraction"></a>
We next estimate the average energy of the core slices. The localization error is $O_t(1)$ by <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a> at offset zero. In the attractive term of <a href="#eq:loc-cut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loc-cut">[eq:loc-cut]</a>, retain only the core repulsion from particles with radius less than $t/4$, which are core with probability one. Conditional integration followed by averaging the original cut labels gives $$\label{a:eq:tail-deletion-attraction}
 \mathbb E\sum_{i\ {\rm out}}\Phi_X(x_i)
 \le\mathbb E_{\Psi_*}\sum_i o(x_i)^2 B_{t/4}(x_i).$$ This comparison of expectations uses the raw inner particles; it does not identify fields from different core slices.

Divide the nonzero terms on the right into shells $u\le|x_i|<2u$, $u=2^\ell t$, $\ell\ge0$. For each shell use an auxiliary cut of the original state, full out on that shell, supported in $u/2<|x|<4u$, with gradient cost $C u^{-2}$, and call its data $Y$. All weighted shell positions and their weights $o(x_i)^2$ are then recorded. Conditional integration of the raw field, with the harmonic version of Lemma <a href="#a:lem:tail-harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:lem:tail-harmonic">48</a>, bounds the shell expectation by $\mathbb E[n_u M(Y)]$, where $n_u=\#\{u\le|x_i|<2u\}$. Equations <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a> and <a href="#a:eq:tail-deletion-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-deletion-field">[a:eq:tail-deletion-field]</a> give $$\mathbb E[n_uM]\le\|n_u\|_2\|M\|_2
 \le C_t\frac{1+\ell}{2^\ell t}.$$ The constants are independent of the shell index and this series is summable. For each fixed system the original attraction and repulsion expectations are finite by the form bounds, so the shell decomposition is legitimate; alternatively one can first use finitely many shells and pass to the limit. Dropping the nonnegative out kinetic energy and out–out repulsion from <a href="#eq:loc-cut" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:loc-cut">[eq:loc-cut]</a>, and using <a href="#a:eq:tail-deletion-attraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-deletion-attraction">[a:eq:tail-deletion-attraction]</a>, now proves <a id="a:eq:tail-deletion-energy"></a>$$\label{a:eq:tail-deletion-energy}
 0\le\mathbb E(e_X-E)\le C_t.$$

Every slice satisfies $e_X\ge E$, and on $G$ sector monotonicity gives $e_X\ge E_{n_c}\ge E_{Z-1}$. Therefore $$c_t(E_{Z-1}-E)
 \le\mathbb P(G)(E_{Z-1}-E)
 \le\mathbb E(e_X-E)\le C_t,$$ which proves <a href="#a:eq:tail-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-offset">[a:eq:tail-offset]</a>. This argument has used only the zero-offset ground state and the general-offset screening estimates; no neutral offset or ionization gap was assumed. ◻

</details>

<a id="a:eq:tail-lower"></a>
By monotonicity, $E_Z\le E_{Z-1}\le E+C$. We may consequently apply Proposition <a href="#a:prop:prop-deficit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:prop-deficit">46</a> again, now with this fixed value of $D_0$, to every normalized neutral ground state. There are universal $s_*>0$ and $Z_1$ such that $$\label{a:eq:tail-lower}
 \int_{|x|\ge s_*}\rho_{\Psi_Z}(x)\,\,\mathrm dx>\frac12
 \qquad (Z\ge Z_1).$$ In fact the deficit proposition gives a tail greater than one at an appropriate such radius. Absolute continuity of the density makes the choice between strict and non-strict radial endpoints immaterial.

<a id="a:sec:gap"></a>
### A uniform positive ionization gap

The energy bound of Section <a href="#a:sec:neutral" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:sec:neutral">12</a> makes a distant insertion trial effective in every singly ionized atom. Averaging the insertion center recovers its unit unscreened charge; a complementary localized state controls the attraction lost when a hole is made for the orbital.

<a id="stmt-a-prop-tail-gap"></a>
<div id="a:prop:tail-gap" class="proposition">

<a id="a:eq:tail-gap"></a>
**Proposition 51** (Positive ionization energy). *There are universal constants $c>0$ and $Z_2<\infty$ such that $$\label{a:eq:tail-gap}
 E_{Z-1}-E_Z\ge c\qquad(Z\ge Z_2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-prop-tail-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-prop-tail-gap</summary>

*Proof.* For $Z\ge\max(Z_0,2)$, take a normalized ground state $\Phi$ of $H_{Z-1,Z}$. It exists by Lemma <a href="#lem:atomic-binding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:atomic-binding">8</a>, and its offset from $E$ is bounded by Proposition <a href="#a:prop:tail-offset" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:tail-offset">50</a>. In this proof $S\ge1$ will be a large universal constant, fixed at the end, and $b=S^{3/5}$. Angle brackets denote normalized Lebesgue averaging over the centers $\mathcal A_S=\{y:S\le|y|\le2S\}$.

<a id="a:eq:tail-insertion"></a>
Use the real normalized radial packet $g_b$ from Section <a href="#sec:localization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:localization">3</a>. Choose smooth real functions $c_y,o_y$ with $c_y^2+o_y^2=1$, $c_y=0$ on $B(y,2b)$, $c_y=1$ outside $B(y,3b)$, and $$|\nabla c_y|^2+|\nabla o_y|^2
 \le Cb^{-2}\mathbf 1_{B(y,3b)}.$$ Set $$F_y=\prod_{i=1}^{Z-1}c_y(x_i),\quad
 n_y^{\rm near}=\#\{i:|x_i-y|<3b\},\quad
 N_y=\mathbb E_\Phi n_y^{\rm near},\quad
 A_y=\mathbb E_\Phi F_y^2.$$ The unnormalized core $F_y\Phi$ has no particles in $B(y,2b)$. Wedge this core with the normalized one-spin orbital $g_b(\cdot-y)$; the resulting unnormalized $Z$-electron vector has squared norm $A_y$. Spatial separation makes the terms assigning different particle labels to the two groups orthogonal. It also makes its energy equal to the core energy, plus the orbital energy times $A_y$, plus the direct cross repulsion. In particular, with the raw-configuration field $$H_y^{\rm add}
 =\int g_b(x-y)^2
       \left(V(x)-\sum_{i=1}^{Z-1}K(x-x_i)\right)\,\,\mathrm dx,$$ the multiplier identity <a href="#eq:sector-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-multiplier">[eq:sector-multiplier]</a> and the variational principle give $$\begin{aligned}
 (E_Z-E_{Z-1})A_y
 &\le \frac12\mathbb E_\Phi|\nabla F_y|^2
       +\frac{\|\nabla g\|_2^2}{2b^2}A_y
       -\mathbb E_\Phi[F_y^2H_y^{\rm add}]\notag\\
 &\le Cb^{-2}(1+N_y)-\mathbb E_\Phi[F_y^2H_y^{\rm add}].
 \label{a:eq:tail-insertion}
\end{aligned}$$ This inequality also holds if $A_y=0$, since its derivation uses the unnormalized trial and its zero norm.

<a id="a:eq:tail-unit-charge"></a>
For every raw configuration, averaging the full insertion field gives $$\label{a:eq:tail-unit-charge}
 \langle H_y^{\rm add}\rangle\ge\frac1{2S}.$$ To verify this without a symmetry assumption on $\Phi$, fix the center radius $r\in[S,2S]$. The nuclear packet average is $Z/r$ since $b<r$. For any fixed electron position $z$, the spherical average in $y$ of its packet potential is $$\int g_b(v)^2\frac1{\max(r,|z-v|)}\,\,\mathrm dv\le\frac1r.$$ This follows by first averaging $K(y+v-z)$ over $|y|=r$ and then integrating $v$. There are $Z-1$ electrons, so the averaged field is at least $1/r\ge1/(2S)$. Radial averaging proves <a href="#a:eq:tail-unit-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-unit-charge">[a:eq:tail-unit-charge]</a>.

<a id="a:eq:tail-insertion-overlap"></a>
The mean number of particles near a proposed center is small. For large $S$, $3b<S/2$, and a particle can lie in $B(y,3b)$ for some $y\in\mathcal A_S$ only if $S/2\le|x_i|\le3S$. The fraction of such centers for any particle is at most $Cb^3/S^3$. Fubini and <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a>, with the bounded offset of $\Phi$, yield $$\label{a:eq:tail-insertion-overlap}
 \langle N_y\rangle
 \le C(b/S)^3\mathbb E_\Phi\#\{i:S/2\le|x_i|\le3S\}
 \le C(b/S)^3(1+\sqrt S)
 \le CS^{-7/10}.$$ Writing $p_y=1-A_y=\mathbb E_\Phi(1-F_y^2)$, the product inequality $1-\prod_i c_y(x_i)^2\le\sum_i(1-c_y(x_i)^2)$ gives $p_y\le N_y$.

<a id="a:eq:tail-complement-gradient"></a>
It remains to bound the potentially positive field lost on the discarded configurations. The symmetric multiplier $G_y=\sqrt{1-F_y^2}$ is Lipschitz, even where it vanishes, and $$\label{a:eq:tail-complement-gradient}
 |\nabla G_y|^2\le Cb^{-2}n_y^{\rm near}.$$ For a direct justification, take all products of $c_y$ and $o_y$ in the particle variables except the all-core product. Their Euclidean norm is $G_y$. The squared gradient of a norm is bounded by the sum of the squared gradients of its components; the full product partition has squared gradient sum $\sum_i(|\nabla c_y|^2+|\nabla o_y|^2)(x_i)$. This proves <a href="#a:eq:tail-complement-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-complement-gradient">[a:eq:tail-complement-gradient]</a> and the stated Lipschitz property.

<a id="a:eq:tail-loss-bound"></a>
If $p_y>0$, put $\Phi_y^{\rm loss}=G_y\Phi/\sqrt{p_y}$. This is a normalized antisymmetric form-domain vector. By <a href="#eq:sector-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-multiplier">[eq:sector-multiplier]</a>, <a href="#a:eq:tail-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-offset">[a:eq:tail-offset]</a>, and <a href="#a:eq:tail-complement-gradient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-complement-gradient">[a:eq:tail-complement-gradient]</a>, its offset satisfies <a id="a:eq:tail-complement-offset"></a>$$\label{a:eq:tail-complement-offset}
 \delta_y\le C+Cb^{-2}N_y/p_y.$$ Choose $S$ large enough also that $b<A S/L$. At each center $y$ in the annulus, the nucleus and every electron included in $J_y$ lie outside the radial packet. Their packet averages equal their point potentials at $y$. All omitted electron terms enter with a negative sign, whence $H_y^{\rm add}\le J_y$ pointwise in the configuration. Using <a href="#a:eq:screen-local" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-local">[a:eq:screen-local]</a> with no initial cut, now in the state $\Phi_y^{\rm loss}$, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_\Phi[(1-F_y^2)H_y^{\rm add}]
 &=p_y\mathbb E_{\Phi_y^{\rm loss}}H_y^{\rm add}\notag\\
 &\le Cp_y\big(S^{-1}+\sqrt{\delta_y}\,S^{-1/2}\big)\notag\\
 &\le C\left[
 p_y(S^{-1}+S^{-1/2})
 +b^{-1}S^{-1/2}\sqrt{p_yN_y}\right].
 \label{a:eq:tail-loss-bound}
\end{aligned}$$ Here we may assume $S/L\ge1$; $L$ is fixed. If $p_y=0$, the left side is zero and the same bound holds. All expectations in this calculation are finite for a fixed system by the form bounds (the packet potential is bounded as well).

Since $p_y\le N_y$, <a href="#a:eq:tail-insertion-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-insertion-overlap">[a:eq:tail-insertion-overlap]</a> implies $$\left\langle\mathbb E_\Phi[(1-F_y^2)H_y^{\rm add}]\right\rangle
 \le C\left(S^{-17/10}+S^{-6/5}+S^{-9/5}\right)
 =o(S^{-1}).$$ Also $$Cb^{-2}(1+\langle N_y\rangle)
 \le C(S^{-6/5}+S^{-19/10})=o(S^{-1}).$$ These estimates explain the width choice: any $b=S^a$ with $1/2<a<2/3$ would make the kinetic term and the largest averaged field loss smaller than $S^{-1}$. All these limits are uniform in $Z$ and in the ground state $\Phi$. Average <a href="#a:eq:tail-insertion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-insertion">[a:eq:tail-insertion]</a> and write $F_y^2H_y^{\rm add}=H_y^{\rm add}-(1-F_y^2)H_y^{\rm add}$. Equations <a href="#a:eq:tail-unit-charge" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-unit-charge">[a:eq:tail-unit-charge]</a> and <a href="#a:eq:tail-loss-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-loss-bound">[a:eq:tail-loss-bound]</a> then show, for one sufficiently large universal choice of $S$, $$(E_Z-E_{Z-1})\langle A_y\rangle\le-\frac1{4S}.$$ In particular $\langle A_y\rangle>0$. As it is at most one, dividing gives $E_{Z-1}-E_Z\ge1/(4S)$. This proves <a href="#a:eq:tail-gap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-gap">[a:eq:tail-gap]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-prop-tail-gap-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-prop-tail-gap</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:first-ionization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:first-ionization">3</a>.* In the notation $E_Z(n)=E(n,Z)$ of the corollary, Proposition <a href="#a:prop:tail-offset" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:tail-offset">50</a> gives universal $C_0,Z_0<\infty$ such that, for $Z\ge Z_0$, $$I_1(Z)=E_Z(Z-1)-E_Z(Z)
 \le E_Z(Z-1)-\inf_{n\ge0}E_Z(n)\le C_0.$$ The first inequality uses $\inf_{n\ge0}E_Z(n)\le E_Z(Z)$. Proposition <a href="#a:prop:tail-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:tail-gap">51</a> gives universal $c_0>0$ and $Z_2<\infty$ such that $I_1(Z)\ge c_0$ for $Z\ge Z_2$.

Choose an integer $Z_*\ge\max\{2,Z_0,Z_2\}$. For each integer $1\le Z<Z_*$, the finite-sector Coulomb forms are bounded below and admit finite-energy trial states, so $E_Z(Z-1)$ and $E_Z(Z)$ are finite; the vacuum energy is $E_Z(0)=0$. Lemma <a href="#lem:atomic-binding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:atomic-binding">8</a>, applied with $n=Z$, gives $I_1(Z)>0$. Thus all the finitely many remaining gaps lie in $(0,\infty)$. Set $$c=\min\{c_0,I_1(1),\ldots,I_1(Z_*-1)\},
 \qquad
 C=\max\{C_0,I_1(1),\ldots,I_1(Z_*-1)\}.$$ These constants satisfy $0<c\le C<\infty$. They are universal because $Z_*$ is universal and the exceptional gaps belong to finitely many atoms in the fixed normalization. The large-$Z$ bounds and these choices prove the assertion for every integer $Z\ge1$. ◻

</details>

<a id="a:sec:tail"></a>
### The outer tail and the half-electron radius

We now combine the positive ionization gap with a weighted shell estimate. The conditional field is estimated after changing the law by the shell mass itself; the measurability of this mass in the cut data is what avoids an additional particle-count factor.

<a id="stmt-a-prop-tail-mass"></a>
<div id="a:prop:tail-mass" class="proposition">

<a id="a:eq:tail-mass"></a>
**Proposition 52** (Uniform exterior mass). *There exist universal $C,R_0,Z_3<\infty$ such that every normalized neutral ground state with $Z\ge Z_3$ satisfies $$\label{a:eq:tail-mass}
 \int_{|x|>R}\rho_{\Psi_Z}(x)\,\,\mathrm dx\le\frac C R
 \qquad(R\ge R_0).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-prop-tail-mass"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-prop-tail-mass</summary>

*Proof.* Fix any such ground state $\Psi=\Psi_Z$ and write $\mathbb E$ for its raw configuration expectation. By Proposition <a href="#a:prop:tail-offset" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:tail-offset">50</a> its offset is bounded by a universal constant, and by Proposition <a href="#a:prop:tail-gap" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:tail-gap">51</a> its ionization gap is at least $c>0$. For $u\ge1$, choose a smooth radial cutoff $\chi_u(r)=\chi(r/u)$, where $0\le\chi\le1$, $\chi=1$ on $[1,2]$, and $\chi$ is supported in $[1/2,4]$. Put $$\begin{gathered}
 w_i=\chi_u(|x_i|)^2,\qquad
 S_u=\sum_{i=1}^Z w_i,\qquad m_u=\mathbb ES_u,\\
 n_{\rm adj}=\#\{i:u/2\le|x_i|\le4u\},\qquad
 N_u=\mathbb En_{\rm adj}.
 \end{gathered}$$ In particular $m_u\le N_u$. Define $$\mathcal B_u(x)=V(x)-\sum_{|x_j|<u/8}K(x-x_j)
        =B_{u/8}(x).$$ Only its values on the support of $\chi_u$ will be used, so the selected electron never occurs among these inner sources.

<a id="a:eq:tail-weighted"></a>
Testing the weak eigen-equation by $S_u\Psi$, and decomposing the result into its $i$-th weighted terms, gives a useful lower bound. For fixed $x_i$ and spin, the other $Z-1$ variables are antisymmetric; their energy is at least $E_{Z-1}$ times their squared norm. The selected electron’s kinetic pairing is $$\operatorname{Re}\frac12\int
 \nabla_i\overline\Psi\cdot\nabla_i(w_i\Psi)
 =\frac12\int w_i|\nabla_i\Psi|^2
       -\frac14\int(\Delta_iw_i)|\Psi|^2
 \ge-Cu^{-2}\mathbb E\mathbf 1_{\{u/2\le|x_i|\le4u\}}.$$ The integration by parts follows first for smooth form approximants; $w_i$ and its first two derivatives are bounded, so it passes to the form domain. Keeping only the selected electron’s repulsion from inner sources, and discarding its other nonnegative pair terms, therefore gives $$\label{a:eq:tail-weighted}
 c m_u\le C u^{-2}N_u
       +\mathbb E\sum_i w_i \mathcal B_u(x_i).$$

<a id="a:eq:tail-biased-offset"></a>
Suppose first that $m_u>0$. The normalized antisymmetric state $$\Psi_u^*=\sqrt{S_u/m_u}\,\Psi$$ is form admissible. Indeed $\sqrt{S_u}$ is the Euclidean norm of the vector $(\chi_u(|x_i|))_i$, hence is Lipschitz. Where $S_u>0$, $$|\nabla\sqrt{S_u}|^2
 =\frac{\sum_i\chi_u(|x_i|)^2|\nabla_i\chi_u(|x_i|)|^2}{S_u}
 \le C u^{-2};$$ its gradient vanishes almost everywhere on its zero set. In particular this is bounded by $Cu^{-2}n_{\rm adj}$. Identity <a href="#eq:sector-multiplier" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sector-multiplier">[eq:sector-multiplier]</a> shows that its offset satisfies $$\label{a:eq:tail-biased-offset}
 \delta_u\le C+Cu^{-2}N_u/m_u<\infty.$$ There is no assumed positive lower bound on $m_u$.

<a id="a:eq:tail-joint-bias"></a>
<a id="a:eq:tail-conditional-bias"></a>
We reveal every position carrying a positive shell weight so that $S_u$ is determined by the data. Use an auxiliary cut which is full out on $u/2\le|x|\le4u$, supported in $u/4<|x|<8u$, and has squared gradient cost at most $Cu^{-2}$. Let $\omega$ denote the raw configuration, let $\ell$ denote these cut labels, and let $Y=Y(\omega,\ell)$ be all recorded out data. If $Q(\,\mathrm d\ell\mid\omega)$ is the conditional label kernel, use exactly this kernel for both states. Their joint laws are then $$\label{a:eq:tail-joint-bias}
 \begin{split}
 P(\,\mathrm d\omega,\,\mathrm d\ell)
  &=|\Psi(\omega)|^2\,\,\mathrm d\omega\,Q(\,\mathrm d\ell\mid\omega),\\
 P^*(\,\mathrm d\omega,\,\mathrm d\ell)
  &=\frac{S_u(\omega)}{m_u}\,P(\,\mathrm d\omega,\,\mathrm d\ell).
 \end{split}$$ Integration in $\omega$ includes the spin sum. Every particle with positive $w_i$ is recorded by this cut, so $$S_u(\omega)=s(Y):=
 \sum_{i\ {\rm recorded}}\chi_u(|x_i|)^2.$$ This equality includes the random labels in the transition regions: particles there have weight zero. It follows from <a href="#a:eq:tail-joint-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-joint-bias">[a:eq:tail-joint-bias]</a> that $$\label{a:eq:tail-conditional-bias}
 \frac{\,\mathrm dP_Y^*}{\,\mathrm dP_Y}=\frac{s(Y)}{m_u},\qquad
 \mathbb E_{P^*}[F\mid Y]=\mathbb E_P[F\mid Y]\quad\text{on }\{s>0\}$$ for every bounded measurable $F$, and then for integrable $F$ by truncation wherever the conditional expectations are defined. For example, multiply the asserted equality by $s(Y)$ and integrate over an arbitrary data event to check it directly. At the slice level the same fact says that $\sqrt{S_u/m_u}$ is constant in the surviving core variables and cancels when their wavefunction is normalized. The set $s=0$ has zero $P^*$-mass and contributes nothing to the original weighted sum.

Let $$h_Y(x)=\mathbb E_P[\mathcal B_u(x)\mid Y],\qquad
 M(Y)=\sup_{u/2\le|x|\le4u}(h_Y(x))_+.$$ On the broad annulus $u/4<|x|<6u$ all the inner sources are separated from the evaluation point. Lemma <a href="#a:lem:tail-harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:lem:tail-harmonic">48</a> supplies continuous harmonic versions of these fields. Applying <a href="#a:eq:tail-conditional-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-conditional-bias">[a:eq:tail-conditional-bias]</a> first at a countable dense set of points and then using continuity makes the conditional fields for $P$ and $P^*$ equal on the entire broad annulus, outside one data-null set on $\{s>0\}$. The two data laws are equivalent on that set. Thus the equality applies at recorded random positions as well as to the positive supremum.

Use <a href="#a:eq:tail-distant-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-distant-field">[a:eq:tail-distant-field]</a> in the state $\Psi_u^*$. It gives <a id="a:eq:tail-biased-field"></a>$$\label{a:eq:tail-biased-field}
 \|M\|_{L^2(P^*)}
 \le C\big(u^{-1}+\sqrt{\delta_u}\,u^{-1/2}\big).$$ This is an application of the screening estimate for arbitrary finite offsets, not of its small-offset refinement. In particular the cut condition is uniform even if $m_u$ is very small: as checked in Lemma <a href="#a:lem:tail-harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:lem:tail-harmonic">48</a>, its cell coefficients obey $D_z^2/(a_zm_z)\le C$ for every $\delta_u$, because $a_zm_z\ge1$.

<a id="a:eq:tail-weighted-field"></a>
To see explicitly how the weighted count is used, introduce the data-measurable measure $$\eta_Y=\sum_{i\ {\rm recorded}}w_i\delta_{x_i},
 \qquad \eta_Y(\mathbb R^3)=s(Y).$$ Conditional integration and <a href="#a:eq:tail-joint-bias" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-joint-bias">[a:eq:tail-joint-bias]</a> now yield $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\sum_iw_i\mathcal B_u(x_i)
 &=\mathbb E_P\int h_Y\,\,\mathrm d\eta_Y\notag\\
 &=m_u\mathbb E_{P^*}
      \left[\frac1{s(Y)}\int h_Y\,\,\mathrm d\eta_Y\right]\notag\\
 &\le m_u\mathbb E_{P^*}M
 \le C m_u\big(u^{-1}+\sqrt{\delta_u}\,u^{-1/2}\big).
 \label{a:eq:tail-weighted-field}
\end{aligned}$$ The quotient may be defined as zero on $s=0$. This exact change of measure accounts for all shell weights, so there is no further particle-count factor in the last bound.

Substituting <a href="#a:eq:tail-biased-offset" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-biased-offset">[a:eq:tail-biased-offset]</a> into <a href="#a:eq:tail-weighted-field" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-weighted-field">[a:eq:tail-weighted-field]</a>, and then into <a href="#a:eq:tail-weighted" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-weighted">[a:eq:tail-weighted]</a>, gives $$\begin{aligned}
 c m_u
 &\le Cu^{-2}N_u
    +Cm_u(u^{-1}+u^{-1/2})
    +Cu^{-3/2}\sqrt{m_uN_u}\\
 &\le C(u^{-2}+u^{-3/2})N_u
    +Cm_u(u^{-1}+u^{-1/2}).
\end{aligned}$$ The second line uses $m_u\le N_u$. When $m_u=0$ it holds trivially without introducing a biased law. For all sufficiently large $u$, absorb the last term into the left side. Applying <a href="#a:eq:screen-annular" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:screen-annular">[a:eq:screen-annular]</a> in the original state, whose offset is uniformly bounded, gives $N_u\le C(1+\sqrt u)$ and hence <a id="a:eq:tail-shell-mass"></a>$$\label{a:eq:tail-shell-mass}
 \mathbb E\#\{i:u\le|x_i|\le2u\}\le m_u\le C/u.$$ Finally, sum over $u=2^jR$, $j\ge0$. The density gives zero mass to the intervening spheres, and $\sum_{j\ge0}(2^jR)^{-1}=2/R$. This proves <a href="#a:eq:tail-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-mass">[a:eq:tail-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-a-prop-tail-mass-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-a-prop-tail-mass</summary>

*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:radius" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:radius">2</a>.* For all sufficiently large $Z$, <a href="#a:eq:tail-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:tail-lower">[a:eq:tail-lower]</a> supplies a fixed $s_*>0$ for which the exterior mass exceeds $1/2$. Monotonicity of that mass as a function of the radius implies $R(\Psi_Z)\ge s_*$. Proposition <a href="#a:prop:tail-mass" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:tail-mass">52</a> supplies a fixed large $R_*$ for which the exterior mass is at most $1/2$, and hence $R(\Psi_Z)\le R_*$.

For clarity we give uniform arguments over the entire ground-state space for each of the finitely many remaining charges. Fix such a $Z$. The strict gap $E_Z<E_{Z-1}$ and the compactness conclusion in Lemma <a href="#lem:atomic-binding" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:atomic-binding">8</a> show that every sequence of normalized neutral ground states has a strongly $L^2$ convergent subsequence. In particular their configuration laws are uniformly tight. To see directly why compactness gives the required uniform tail, suppose otherwise and choose radii $R_k\to\infty$ and normalized ground states $\Psi_k$ with $$\int_{|x|>R_k}\rho_{\Psi_k}(x)\,\,\mathrm dx>\frac12.$$ After a subsequence $\Psi_k\to\Psi$ strongly in $L^2$. The bounded multiplication operator $\sum_i\mathbf 1_{\{|x_i|>R_k\}}$ has norm at most $Z$, so its expectations in $\Psi_k$ and $\Psi$ differ by at most $2Z\|\Psi_k-\Psi\|_2$. Its expectation in the fixed $\Psi$ tends to zero by dominated convergence, a contradiction. Thus a finite upper radius works uniformly for this $Z$.

For the lower bound, the kinetic estimate <a href="#eq:atomic-global-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:atomic-global-density">[eq:atomic-global-density]</a>, with the fixed finite offset $E_Z-E$, gives $$\int\rho_{\Psi_Z}^{5/3}\le C_Z$$ uniformly over its normalized ground states. Hölder’s inequality therefore yields $$\int_{|x|<r}\rho_{\Psi_Z}(x)\,\,\mathrm dx
 \le |B(0,r)|^{2/5}
       \left(\int\rho_{\Psi_Z}^{5/3}\right)^{3/5}
 \le C_Z r^{6/5}.$$ Choose a positive $r_Z$ making this less than $Z-1/2$. The exterior mass then exceeds $1/2$, uniformly over the ground states, so $R(\Psi_Z)\ge r_Z$.

Taking the minimum of $s_*$ and these finitely many positive lower radii, and the maximum of $R_*$ and the finitely many upper radii, gives universal $0<c\le C<\infty$. All density measures used above are absolutely continuous. Their exterior masses are consequently continuous in the radius, start at $Z$, and tend to zero. Thus the infimum defining the half-electron radius has the stated bounds for every normalized neutral ground state, as required. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:interfaces"></a>
### Interfaces for atomic asymptotics

The estimates used in questions about ionization energies and outer radii have different offset requirements. We collect their precise scope here; the complete proofs are in the preceding sections.

<a id="pm-h-63"></a>
###### Arbitrary energy offsets.

For a finite-sector state of energy at most $E+\delta$, the exact cut identity, coherent insertion, and fine-density lower bounds are Lemmas <a href="#lem:loc-identity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-identity">13</a>, <a href="#lem:loc-hole-trial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-hole-trial">14</a>, and <a href="#lem:loc-cubes" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:loc-cubes">15</a>. In atomic distance geometry, Proposition <a href="#a:prop:screen-local" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:screen-local">20</a> and Corollary <a href="#a:cor:screen-annular" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:cor:screen-annular">24</a> allow every finite $\delta\ge0$; their count unit is $\max(a^{-3},1)+\sqrt{\delta a}$. For sectors $n\le3Z$, Lemma <a href="#a:lem:tail-harmonic" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:lem:tail-harmonic">48</a> keeps this dependence in its complete convergent series for conditional inner fields. The fine comparison Lemma <a href="#a:cor:screen-refined" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:cor:screen-refined">21</a> has the additional restriction $\delta\le a^{-7+.02}$. These are distinct assertions.

<a id="pm-h-64"></a>
###### Events of a sector ground state.

For a ground state of energy $E_n$, Lemma <a href="#lem:obs-tilt" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:obs-tilt">26</a> bounds the cost above $E_n$ by $C\ell_j^{-2}\log^5(e/p)$, independent of the number of particles or arrays. The offset above $E$ is therefore $E_n-E+C\ell_j^{-2}\log^5(e/p)$. This exact baseline dependence is available when the offset grows. The fixed-offset comparison Proposition <a href="#a:prop:cond-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:cond-comparison">45</a>, by contrast, first fixes a finite $D_0\ge E_n-E$, then takes $s$ sufficiently small according to $D_0$ and the stated band and accuracy parameters. It is not a uniform comparison at arbitrary growing $D_0$.

<a id="pm-h-65"></a>
###### Common observations and intermediate fields.

The master-kernel formula and its universal constants in <a href="#a:eq:cond-master" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="a:eq:cond-master">[a:eq:cond-master]</a> do not depend on the inverse-comparison heights or accuracy. To use several comparisons with the same conditional density and field, choose $s$ to satisfy all their corresponding smallness conditions in Proposition <a href="#a:prop:cond-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="a:prop:cond-comparison">45</a>, then fix the resulting packet widths and observation arrays. This applies to any collection admitting such a common $s$; for finitely many fixed requests, the minimum of their thresholds suffices.
